Vanu v Minister for Immigration and Citizenship

22 Oct 2026Federal Court of Australia[2026] FCA 1581Migration: court challengeTribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewMigration
The applicant, a New Zealand citizen whose visa was mandatorily cancelled after a criminal conviction, sought an urgent injunction to stop his removal to New Zealand while he sought an extension of time to challenge the Tribunal’s non-revocation decision. Horan J refused the injunction because the proposed review grounds were unparticularised and no serious question was shown, with the balance of convenience also against relief.

DXJL and Minister for Immigration and Citizenship (Migration)

21 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2436Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
DXJL, the holder of a Global Special Humanitarian (Class XB) (Subclass 202) visa, had his visa mandatorily cancelled and a delegate refused to revoke that cancellation. The Administrative Review Tribunal affirmed the non-revocation decision, finding he did not pass the character test and that Direction No. 110 considerations did not justify revocation.
Key holding

Youth Court offences initially dealt with without conviction may be taken into account in a Direction 110 character assessment where they are later re-sentenced with convictions and detention.

I am therefore satisfied that respective convictions were recorded for each of these 29 offences and that all of them can be taken into account for present purposes.

Gibson and Chief Executive Officer, National Disability Insurance Agency (Practice and procedure)

20 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2437Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
The Tribunal refused an application to appoint NDIS participant Shannon Gibson’s support coordinator, Geeta Carroll, as his litigation supporter in a Tribunal review about his NDIS plan. Although the Tribunal accepted that Mr Gibson lacked decision-making ability, it was not satisfied that the appointment was “necessary” because his representative, plan-nominee mother, interpreter, Tribunal adjustments and informal supports already allowed participation.
Key holding

Under s 67(1)(b) of the Administrative Review Tribunal Act 2024 (Cth), a litigation supporter is not “necessary” merely because appointment is desirable, expedient, convenient or preferable; it is a last-resort measure required only where available and suitable other measures do not allow the party to participate.

It follows that the use of the word necessary has the effect that an appointment that would make the proceeding easier or more convenient to manage, or that the parties would welcome, is not for that reason 'necessary'. The choice of the word emphasises that an appointment is a measure of last resort, made only where the party cannot otherwise participate.

Lippl and CEO, National Disability Insurance Agency (NDIS)

20 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2416Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
This NDIS review concerned whether Ms Lippl should receive additional 1:1 support worker hours beyond those in her statement of participant supports. The Tribunal was not satisfied the requested additional hours represented value for money, finding her current flexible supports sufficient and an increase risked dependency. It nonetheless set aside and remitted the matter to fix the SoPS wording and specify support-worker assistance in accordance with the orders.
Key holding

Additional requested NDIS support worker hours are not reasonable and necessary where the evidence shows the participant can use current flexible funding to meet needs and increased hours risk dependency, so the value-for-money criterion is not met.

The Tribunal has determined the requested additional support worker hours do not satisfy the statutory requirement of representing value for money and cannot be approved by the Tribunal. The evidence is that Ms Lippl can use her support worker hours flexibly, that the current provision is sufficient to meet her needs and that increasing her hours risks creating dependency on support workers over time.

Rahimi and Minister for Immigration and Citizenship (Migration)

20 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2400Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
The Tribunal reviewed a delegate’s decision not to revoke the mandatory cancellation of Mr Rahimi’s Class XB Subclass 201 visa under s 501CA(4). Mr Rahimi failed the character test due to a 15-month aggregate sentence, but the Tribunal set aside the delegate’s decision and revoked the cancellation. The case turned on the Direction No 110 balancing, especially mental health, family support and risk of reoffending.

Australian Securities and Investments Commission v Dawson

20 Oct 2026Federal Court of Australia[2026] FCA 1548Migration: court challengeTribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
SXHR, a Sri Lankan unauthorised maritime arrival in immigration detention, sought judicial review of a Tribunal decision affirming refusal of a Bridging E visa under s 501(1) of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth). The Tribunal had applied Direction No 110 and refused the visa notwithstanding the applicant’s outstanding Ministerial intervention request. The Federal Court dismissed the review.
Key holding

Direction 110 requires comparison of the risk attached to refusal and grant of the visa, but not separate assessment of every possible outcome after grant where only the grant creates a risk of the applicant remaining in the community.

There is no error in that approach; nothing could have been gained by comparing the nature or extent of that risk depending on which of the two identified scenarios following the grant of a visa eventuated. DVRL does not say anything against that.

XMLQ and Minister for Foreign Affairs

19 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2467Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewTorts
XMLQ, a Pakistani national on a Bridging E visa, applied for a Convention Travel Document but was issued a Certificate of Identity instead. On review, the Administrative Review Tribunal held it had no power to determine whether he was a refugee under the Refugee Convention and affirmed the CTD refusal. The decision turns on s 6(1)(b) of the Australian Passports Determination 2015 (Cth).
Key holding

Section 6(1)(b) of the Determination requires an existing recognition as a refugee under the Refugee Convention and does not confer on the Tribunal power to undertake a refugee status determination.

The phrase "is recognised" naturally directs attention to a status that already exists. It describes a condition that must be satisfied before a Convention Travel Document may be issued.

Xi and Minister for Immigration and Citizenship (Migration)

19 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2397Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
The applicant, a Chinese national, had his Resident Return (Class BB) (Subclass 155) visa mandatorily cancelled under s 501(3A) after being sentenced to 48 months’ imprisonment for offences arising from a cybercrime scam syndicate and firearm possession. A delegate refused to revoke the cancellation under s 501CA(4), and the Tribunal affirmed that decision. The Tribunal found the offending serious and that community protection and expectations outweighed a low risk of reoffending and the applicant’s ties.
Key holding

Serious offending may cause the community expectations consideration under Direction No 110 to weigh significantly against revocation despite an applicant’s personal circumstances.

In the present case, the Tribunal has found the applicant's offending is serious. Taking into consideration the personal circumstances of the applicant, it considers this consideration weighs significantly against revocation of the cancellation.

Hoang and Minister for Immigration and Citizenship (Migration)

19 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2396Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
The Tribunal reviewed the Minister’s refusal to revoke the mandatory cancellation of Mr Hoang’s Class BF Transitional (Permanent) visa under s 501(3A) of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth). Although Mr Hoang had a substantial criminal record for repeat drug and weapons offending and failed the character test, the Tribunal found another reason to revoke under s 501CA(4) and set aside the refusal.
Key holding

A residual risk of reoffending may be acceptable, and another reason to revoke may exist, where serious addiction-related offending is offset by sustained rehabilitation, family support and long residence.

The Applicant has, nonetheless, made significant progress in rehabilitation and has retained the support and trust of his immediate family who are an important element to his future success. I consider that the 'residual' risk of reoffending is acceptable despite the potentially serious consequences for the community, and Mr Hoang's length of residence from a young age must take into account.

FWKPM and Chief Executive Officer, National Insurance Disability Agency (Practice and procedure)

16 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2402Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
In FWKPM and Chief Executive Officer, National Disability Insurance Agency (Practice and procedure) [2026] ARTA 2402, an NDIS review required determination of whether three summonses should issue to two schools and a support-coordination provider. A Registrar refused the requests on excessive-scope and privacy grounds, but the Tribunal held the documents were apparently relevant and privacy did not justify refusal. The Tribunal issued all three summonses with a return date of 30 October 2026.
Key holding

Where documents are of apparent relevance and no party objects, the Tribunal should ordinarily exercise its discretion to issue a summons unless the summons is vexatious, frivolous or otherwise an abuse of process.

I would add one further observation, namely that generally speaking where a party asks for a summons, the documents sought are of apparent relevance, and no other party objects to the summons being issued, the discretion to issue a summons should ordinarily be exercised, unless it vexatious or frivolous or otherwise an abuse of process to issue the summons.

Latif and Commissioner of the NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission (Practice and procedure)

16 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2342Tribunal reviewVisa refusal review
The Tribunal refused the NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission’s application to dismiss Mr Latif’s review applications concerning deemed internal review decisions to affirm a permanent ban and registration renewal refusal for Horizon Solsolutions Australia Pty Ltd. The case concerned whether the Tribunal had jurisdiction, whether Mr Latif was “directly affected”, and whether deemed decisions arose under the ART Act. The dismissal application was dismissed, so the substantive review applications remain on foot.
Key holding

A decision-maker cannot isolate a separate non-reviewable “standing” decision from the NDIS Act s 100 internal review process; standing is a threshold matter within that process and may be assessed by the Tribunal in determining jurisdiction.

It is my characterisation that the issue of standing is inherently a threshold matter that should be addressed as an integral part of the internal review process under s 100 of the NDIS Act, rather than as a distinct and, in this case as submitted by the Respondent, a non-reviewable decision made under s 100(2) of that Act.

NPNQY and CEO, National Disability Insurance Agency (Practice and procedure)

15 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2439Tribunal reviewVisa refusal review
The Tribunal refused an interlocutory stay sought by an NDIS participant against a June 2026 statement of participant supports and for reinstatement of the earlier November Plan. Although the Tribunal accepted the applicant had an arguable case and some short-term utility in a stay, it was not satisfied that refusing the stay would deprive her of a full hearing or render a successful review nugatory.
Key holding

A stay under s 32(2) of the ART Act is only granted where desirable to ensure the effectiveness of review, requiring more than hardship or inconvenience and ordinarily requiring proof that the review would be adversely affected by irreversible prejudice or a nugatory outcome.

The power conferred by s 32(2) will only be exercised for a specific statutory purpose where it is necessary to ensure the effectiveness of the review. In other words, it must be established that the review would be adversely affected because the party seeking the stay would suffer irreversible prejudice if the stay sought were not granted, such as where a successful outcome in the substantive review would be rendered nugatory.

Mishra and Minister for Immigration and Citizenship (Migration)

15 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2327Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
The Tribunal reviewed a delegate’s decision not to revoke the mandatory cancellation of Mr Mishra’s Subclass 155 Five-Year Resident Return visa under s 501(3A) of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth). Mr Mishra accepted he did not pass the character test because of a 22-month aggregate sentence for family violence offences. The Tribunal affirmed the non-revocation decision after considering whether another reason existed under s 501CA(4)(b)(ii) and Direction No. 110.
Key holding

Where a visa is mandatorily cancelled because the holder fails the character test due to a substantial criminal record, the Tribunal must consider whether there is another reason to revoke under s 501CA(4)(b)(ii), applying Direction No. 110.

In accordance with s 501CA(4)(b)(ii), the Tribunal must therefore consider whether there is another reason to exercise the discretion under s 501CA(4) to revoke the original decision.

Smith v Minister for Immigration and Citizenship

15 Oct 2026Federal Court of Australia[2026] FCA 1520Migration: court challengeTribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
Mr Smith, a New Zealand citizen, had his Class TY Subclass 444 visa cancelled under s 501(3A) of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth) after criminal convictions and imprisonment. A delegate refused to revoke the cancellation, and the Administrative Review Tribunal affirmed that refusal. On judicial review, the Federal Court found the Tribunal misconstrued Direction 110’s definition of “family violence” and set aside its decision.
Key holding

Conduct falling within an example of family violence in Direction 110 cl 4 is not automatically family violence; it must also have the effect of coercing or controlling a family member or causing that person to be fearful.

For example, behaviour which involves "intentionally damaging or destroying property" will not necessarily constitute family violence. It will only do so if that behaviour had the effect of coercing or controlling a member of the perpetrator's family, or causing the family member or members to be fearful.

De Been and CEO, National Disability Insurance Agency (NDIS)

14 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2344Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewMigration
No plain summary is available for this decision. Read the full text via the link below.
Key holding

A support can be included in a statement of participant supports only if it is an NDIS support under the Transitional Rules and satisfies each cumulative requirement in s 34(1) of the NDIS Act.

Section 34(1) is cumulative and therefore all the criteria must be met. The Transitional Supports Rules, (made under the National Disability Insurance Scheme Amendment (Getting the NDIS back on Track No. 1) Act 2024 (Amending Act) determines whether something is, or is not, a NDIS support as defined at s 10.

CYZGC by his plan nominee and CEO, National Disability Insurance Agency (Practice and procedure)

14 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2332Tribunal reviewVisa refusal review
The applicant, a 10-year-old NDIS participant, sought interim relief after a reassessment increased his core supports but reduced his therapy funding and added behaviour support. He asked the Tribunal under s 32(2) of the Administrative Review Tribunal Act 2024 (Cth) to reallocate or make flexible the behaviour support funding, or alternatively to stay Plan-2 so Plan-1 revived. The Tribunal refused, finding neither order desirable to ensure the effectiveness of the review, and recommended an early conference and hearing.
Key holding

Under s 32(2) of the Administrative Review Tribunal Act 2024 (Cth), an interim stay or other order is made only if desirable to ensure the effectiveness of the review; hardship, even serious hardship, is insufficient unless it puts the effectiveness of the review in jeopardy.

Finally, in relation to hardship, it is not enough that the decision causes hardship, even serious hardship. The discretion may only be exercised where the degree of hardship is such that it puts the effectiveness of the review in jeopardy.[5]

LFQR and Secretary, Department of Social Services (Social security second review)

14 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2320Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewEmployment & workplace
The Tribunal reviewed a refusal to pay family tax benefit top ups and supplements for 2022/23 because the applicant’s partner lodged his income tax return on 4 July 2024, after the 30 June 2024 reconciliation deadline. The applicant relied on stress, family violence, alcohol issues, a brief separation and pandemic-related pressures. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal, finding no special circumstances that prevented the partner from lodging on time.
Key holding

Under s 32D(1)(c)(ii) of the Administration Act, special circumstances will only extend the reconciliation time if they actually prevented the partner from lodging the income tax return by the required date.

For the Applicant to be paid top ups and supplements of family tax benefit for 2022/23, the Tribunal must be satisfied that there are special circumstances that prevented the Applicant's partner from lodging his income tax return by 30 June 2024.

Jaworski v Chartered Accountants Australia and New Zealand (No 2)

14 Oct 2026New South Wales Court of Appeal[2026] NSWCA 213Migration: court challengeTribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Ward P; Harrison AJA Decision: 1. Dismiss the applicant’s application to set asi
Mr Jaworski sought to set aside or vary the Court of Appeal’s earlier leave judgment under UCPR r 36.16, and CA ANZ sought gross sum costs. The Court dismissed the reopening application and fixed costs at $30,000 for the leave/recusal application and $6,000 for CA ANZ’s notice of motion. The dispute concerned alleged errors, bias/prejudgment and costs in ongoing litigation involving CA ANZ.
Key holding

Under UCPR r 36.16, reopening requires some error in the court's reasoning due to misapprehension of fact or law that is not solely attributable to the applicant's neglect or default.

What is required to be established on an application under r 36.16 is some error in the court's reasoning because of a misapprehension of the facts or misapprehension of the relevant law; and that this misapprehension cannot be attributed solely to the neglect or default of the party seeking the rehearing.

Coe and Great Barrier Reef Marine Park Authority

13 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2317Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewCriminal law
Jason Coe applied to have Jardini Pty Ltd’s Great Barrier Reef Marine Park permit transferred to him personally. The Authority refused, and on review the Tribunal found he was a suitable person but still affirmed refusal because other matters under s 120(c) of the GBRMP Regulations concerned the objects of the GBRMP Act and orderly management of the Marine Park. The Tribunal also resolved procedural issues about production, redactions and deciding on the papers.
Key holding

Even where the Tribunal does not find a proposed transferee unsuitable, it may refuse transfer under s 120(c) because of other matters relevant to the objects of the GBRMP Act or management of the Marine Park.

For the reasons set out in more detail below, the Tribunal has not found the Applicant to be an unsuitable person. However it does believe that at this time, and having regard to the present circumstances, there are matters which are relevant to the proposed transfer and the objects of the GBRMP Act and management of the Marine Park such that the transfer of the Permit should not be approved.

WGSK and Minister for Immigration and Citizenship (Migration)

9 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2297Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
WGSK, a Polish national, sought review of a delegate’s refusal of a Protection (subclass 866) visa under s 36(2C)(a)(ii) of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth), on the basis that there were serious reasons for considering he committed a serious non-political crime before entering Australia. The Tribunal found the Polish armed robbery conviction and a pre-entry conspiracy to import cocaine provided serious reasons, while finding the completed import offence did not occur before he entered Australia. It affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

The “serious reasons for considering” threshold in s 36(2C)(a)(ii) requires evidence sufficient to support a strong inference, not proof to the criminal or civil standard.

The qualifying term "serious" indicates that the reasons must be sufficient to support a strong inference.

Niven and Minister for Immigration and Citizenship (Migration)

8 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2274Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
This was a merits review of a delegate’s decision not to revoke the mandatory cancellation of Mr Niven’s Class TY Subclass 444 Special Category (Temporary) visa under s 501CA(4) of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth). The Tribunal found the applicant did not pass the character test but accepted there was another reason to revoke the cancellation, set aside the delegate’s decision, and substituted a decision revoking the cancellation.
Key holding

Under s 501CA(4)(b)(ii), where an applicant fails the character test, the Tribunal may revoke mandatory cancellation if satisfied there is another reason, applying Direction no. 110.

Section 501CA(4) states that the Minister may revoke the original decision if: (a) the person makes representations in accordance with the invitation; and (b) the Minister is satisfied: (i) that the person passes the character test (as defined by s 501); or (ii) that there is another reason why the original decision should be revoked.

Comcare v Administrative Review Tribunal

8 Oct 2026Federal Court of Australia[2026] FCA 1481Migration: court challengeTribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewEmployment & workplace
Comcare sought judicial review under s 39B of the Judiciary Act 1903 (Cth) of an Administrative Review Tribunal decision refusing to release Comcare and Mr Sewell from the Harman obligation over summonsed medical records. Comcare wanted to use the records to manage Mr Sewell’s future SRC Act compensation claims after liability under s 14 was accepted by consent. The Federal Court dismissed the application, holding the Tribunal committed no jurisdictional error.
Key holding

The general functions, powers and decision-making provisions in the SRC Act do not impliedly exclude the Harman obligation for documents produced on summons in ART review proceedings concerning s 14 liability.

For these reasons, ss 69(a), 70 and 72 of the SRC Act (whether alone or in combination) do not establish a statutory inconsistency that would prevent the Harman obligation from applying to documents produced on a summons in Tribunal proceedings to determine liability under s 14 of the SRC Act.

RSZN v Minister for Immigration and Citizenship

8 Oct 2026Federal Court of Australia[2026] FCA 1467Migration: court challengeTribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
The Federal Court judicially reviewed an Administrative Review Tribunal decision affirming the refusal of a Safe Haven Enterprise (subclass 790) visa to an Iraqi former army officer. The Tribunal found serious reasons to consider he committed crimes against humanity by assisting/contributing to the persecution of Kurds during the Anfal campaign, excluding him under ss 5H(2)(a) and 36(2C)(a)(i) of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth). The Court held the Tribunal misunderstood or misapplied the Rome Statute’s accessorial liability requirements.
Key holding

For art 25(3)(c) of the Rome Statute, an accessory must have lent assistance with the aim or purpose of facilitating the offence; mere knowledge that the conduct will assist the principal perpetrator is insufficient.

This wording introduces a higher subjective mental element and means that the accessory must have lent his or her assistance with the aim of facilitating the offence. It is not sufficient that the accessory merely knows that his or her conduct will assist the principal perpetrator in the commission of the offence.

TRPMK and CEO, National Disability Insurance Agency (Practice and procedure)

5 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2322Tribunal reviewVisa refusal review
TRPMK sought review of an NDIA decision refusing access to the National Disability Insurance Scheme. During ART proceedings, she applied for the recusal of Senior Member J Collins alleging apprehended bias, lack of institutional independence and acting under dictation, including based on an alleged relationship between the ART President and her former husband. The Tribunal refused the recusal request; the extract also records a short adjournment of the hearing to 7 August 2026.
Key holding

A decision-maker must recuse if a fair-minded lay observer might reasonably apprehend that the decision-maker might not bring an impartial mind to the resolution of the question.

Under the established test in Ebner v Official Trustee in Bankruptcy [2000] HCA 63, the legal standard is whether a fair-minded lay observer might reasonably apprehend that the decision-maker might not bring an impartial mind to the resolution of the question.

Kintanar (Migration)

2 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2384Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewMigration
In Kintanar (Migration) [2026] ARTA 2384, the Tribunal reviewed a refusal of a Partner (Temporary) (Class UK)/Subclass 820 visa because the applicant’s non-migrating child, Leonel, failed PIC 4007 due to severe cognitive impairment/autism and an estimated care cost of $1,577,840. The Tribunal found the MOC opinion valid but waived the health criterion under PIC 4007(2)(b), weighing the child’s non-migrating status, the applicant’s and sponsor’s regional NSW contributions, and their capacity to mitigate future costs.
Key holding

Under PIC 4007(2)(b), the Tribunal may waive the health criterion where the benefits and contributions of the applicant and sponsor, the detriment of refusal, and the applicant’s capacity to mitigate future costs outweigh a cost that cannot be realised from the grant of the visa alone.

The Tribunal concludes that the benefits that the visa applicant and her partner bring to the Australian community, the detriment to the visa sponsor and his family if the visa was refused and the applicant and sponsor's capacity to mitigate the potential costs to the Australia community should Leonel apply for a visa in the future to join his mother, outweigh a cost to the Australian community which cannot be realised from the grant of this visa alone.

Upson and Minister for Immigration and Citizenship (Migration)

1 Oct 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2275Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
In Upson and Minister for Immigration and Citizenship (Migration) [2026] ARTA 2275, a 31-year-old New Zealand citizen who has lived in Australia since infancy had his special category visa mandatorily cancelled under s 501(3A) after being sentenced to 7 years 6 months for serious drug offences. A delegate refused to revoke the cancellation, but the Administrative Review Tribunal set that decision aside and substituted revocation after finding “another reason” under s 501CA(4)(b)(ii).
Key holding

Under Direction no. 110, whether a non-citizen poses an “unacceptable risk” is a distinct evaluative conclusion reached by balancing the likelihood and potential harm of future offending, not merely the likelihood of re-offending.

The concept of 'risk', and whether it is 'unacceptable', under cl 8.1.2(1) of the Direction is not the same thing as the 'likelihood of the non-citizen engaging in further criminal or other serious conduct'.[25]

Zhou (Migration)

28 Sep 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2365Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Ms Zhou sought review of the refusal of a Student (Temporary) (Class TU) Subclass 500 visa. The delegate refused because it was not satisfied she was a genuine temporary entrant under cl 500.212. By the Tribunal’s decision time she had completed her course and returned home, but because she had no current enrolment, the Tribunal affirmed under cl 500.211.
Key holding

For a Subclass 500 visa, cl 500.211(a) requires current enrolment in a course of study at the time of the Tribunal’s decision, and absence of current enrolment requires affirmation even if the applicant previously studied and completed courses.

However, as she is now not enrolled and cl 500.211 is a time of decision criterion, the Tribunal must affirm the review.

Manpreet Kaur (Migration)

25 Sep 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2386Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewVisa integrityMigration
The Tribunal reviewed a refusal of a Subclass 500 (Student) visa for Ms Kaur, an Indian citizen who has lived in Australia as a student since 2018. Although the Tribunal accepted she was enrolled in a registered course, it was not satisfied she genuinely intended to stay temporarily because her latest lower-level course appeared designed to extend her residence rather than advance her studies. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

Under cl 500.212, all of paras (a), (b) and (c) must be satisfied, so failure to establish cl 500.212(a) alone is sufficient to fail the genuine applicant criterion.

To satisfy cl 500.212, all of subcls (a), (b) and (c) must be met and an unfavourable finding in relation to either (a) or (b) forecloses the possibility of a favourable outcome.

Kulwant KAUR (Migration)

23 Sep 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2351Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
This was a review of a refusal of a Visitor (Class FA) Subclass 600 visa on genuine temporary entrant grounds under cl 600.211. The applicant was a 59-year-old widow from India whose children all live in Australia, and the family sought to keep her onshore so she could later apply for a permanent visa. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal because it was not satisfied she genuinely intended only a temporary stay.
Key holding

The Tribunal’s task under cl 600.211 is to assess whether the applicant genuinely intends a temporary stay for the visa’s purpose, not to facilitate permanent residence.

The Tribunal does not see it as its role to facilitate the applicant's intention to remain in Australia permanently regardless of the requirements of the Visitor visa. The Tribunal's role is to simply assess whether the applicant genuinely intends to stay in Australia temporarily for the purpose for which the visa is granted.

Nyagua and Minister for Immigration and Citizenship (Migration)

22 Sep 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2298Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
This Tribunal review concerns the mandatory cancellation of Ms Nyagua’s Resident Return (Subclass 155) visa after her conviction for importing a marketable quantity of a border-controlled drug. Although she failed the character test, the Tribunal found another reason to revoke the cancellation. The decision under review was set aside and the visa cancellation revoked under s 501CA(4).
Key holding

Where a non-citizen fails the character test after mandatory visa cancellation, the Tribunal may revoke cancellation under s 501CA(4)(b)(ii) if satisfied there is another reason, exercising the discretion in accordance with the applicable ministerial direction.

Section 501CA(4)(b)(ii) of the Act requires the Tribunal to consider if there is another reason why the discretion should be exercised to revoke the original decision. In exercising this discretion, the Tribunal must have regard to the Direction (s 499(2A)).

Hares (Migration)

17 Sep 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2454Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
This was a review of a delegate’s refusal to grant Subclass 309 (Spouse (Provisional)) visas to Mr Mohamad Bali and family, sponsored by Ms Bassima Hares. The Tribunal had to decide whether the couple was in a genuine and continuing married relationship under s 5F of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth) at application and at decision. It affirmed the refusal because, despite accepting a valid marriage, it was not satisfied on the evidence of mutual commitment, a joint household or a genuine continuing relationship.
Key holding

Abundant documentary evidence alone does not establish a genuine spousal relationship; the Tribunal must be positively satisfied of the s 5F(2) elements on all the circumstances.

Documentary evidence in itself, no matter how abundant, does not establish in the Tribunal's view the existence of a genuine spousal relationship.

Gunarathne (Migration)

17 Sep 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2449Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
This was a review of a refusal to grant a New Zealand Citizen Family Relationship (Temporary) (Class UP) Subclass 461 visa. The applicant applied while holding only a Bridging A visa and failed to satisfy the Schedule 3 criteria required because he did not hold a substantive visa. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

Failure to satisfy either of the cumulative requirements in Schedule 3 criterion 3004(d) or 3004(f) prevents an applicant from meeting criterion 3004 as a whole and therefore cl 461.213(b)(ii).

As I am not satisfied the applicant meets 3004(d) or (f), it follows that the applicant does not meet 3004 as a whole. Accordingly, he does not satisfy cl 461.213(b)(ii) of Schedule 2 to the Regulations and cannot meet the requirements for the grant of the visa.

2306518 (Migration)

17 Sep 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2406Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
In 2306518 (Migration) [2026] ARTA 2406, the Tribunal reviewed a refusal of a Partner (Temporary) (Class UK) visa (Subclass 820) after the applicant’s relationship with his sponsor ceased. The applicant claimed to have experienced family violence, but the Tribunal was not satisfied and obtained an Independent Expert opinion that he had not. Because the opinion was validly made, the Tribunal treated it as correct and affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

Where a non-judicially determined family violence claim is referred to an Independent Expert and the opinion is validly made, the Tribunal must accept that opinion as correct and cannot prefer other reports.

I am required to take the opinion of the Independent Expert as correct, unless that opinion is not validly made. The Tribunal cannot decide to give weight to other reports in the circumstances where the Tribunal has referred the matter to an Independent Expert.

Triolo (Migration)

17 Sep 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2358Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
This was a review of a delegate’s refusal to grant a 95-year-old Italian citizen a Visitor (Class FA) visa, Subclass 600. The Tribunal had to decide whether the grant would authorise a stay of more than 12 consecutive months and, if so, whether exceptional circumstances existed under cl 600.215. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal, finding no exceptional circumstances and noting other visitor-visa criteria were also in issue.
Key holding

An intention or desire to remain permanently in Australia, even if reasonable and based on need for care and medical treatment, cannot be an exceptional circumstance for a further temporary visitor visa under cl 600.215.

I also consider that, where a person clearly desires and intends to stay permanently in Australia, (however reasonable and even where it is based on an applicant's need for care and medical treatment as in this case) that desire cannot reasonably be considered to be an exceptional circumstance for the grant of a further temporary visa.

Accordingly, it is appropriate for me to set aside the decision under review and substitute decision that the cancellation decision be revoked.

Lee (Migration)

16 Sep 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2375Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Mr Lee applied for a Student (Temporary) (Class TU) Subclass 500 visa. The delegate refused the visa because he did not meet the English language requirement. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal because his PTE score of 37 required current ELICOS enrolment, and his prior courses did not establish the timing exemption.
Key holding

Where a Subclass 500 applicant relies on a PTE score between 36 and 41, the English language requirement is not satisfied by past ELICOS study alone; the applicant must be enrolled in the required ELICOS as at the date of assessment.

When an applicant has not achieved a minimum PTE test score of 50 and relies on an ELICOS to satisfy the English language requirements, the applicant must be enrolled in the ELICOS as at the date of assessment.

FPXJ and Secretary, Department of Social Services (Practice and procedure)

16 Sep 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2321Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewBankruptcy & insolvency
The Applicant, FPXJ, sought an extension of time to bring second review applications in the Administrative Review Tribunal against two AAT first review decisions dated 19 August 2019 and 29 January 2020 concerning disability support pension debts. The Tribunal rejected his procedural and bias arguments and refused the extension. It found the delay exceeded six years, was not fully explained, and that reopening settled matters would prejudice the Respondent and wider public, with no practical utility in the substantive review.
Key holding

Under s 19 of the ART Act, the Tribunal may refuse an extension of time where significant delay, prejudice and lack of practical utility mean it is not reasonable in all the circumstances to extend time.

Given this, and given the prejudice to the Respondent and to the wider public if an extension of time is granted, the Tribunal has decided that it is not reasonable in all of the circumstances to extend time for the Applicant to make second review applications. The extension of time applications are refused by the Tribunal.

Ututaonga and Minister for Immigration and Citizenship (Migration)

15 Sep 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2285Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
This is a migration review of a delegate’s decision not to revoke the mandatory cancellation of a New Zealand citizen’s Class TY Subclass 444 Special Category (Temporary) visa under s 501CA(4) of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth). The applicant had a substantial criminal record, including serious drug supply offences, and the Tribunal had to weigh the Direction no. 110 considerations for and against revocation.
Key holding

In exercising the s 501CA(4) discretion, the Tribunal must weigh all primary and other considerations under Direction no. 110 in a single evaluation, with greater weight generally given to protection of the Australian community.

In determining the weight to be applied to each consideration, the Tribunal considered the primary and other considerations and weighed them in light of the evidence and findings using the guidance provided by the Direction. Greater weight must generally be given to the protection of the Australian community than other primary considerations.

Kawabata (Migration)

4 Sep 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2453Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewMigration
The Tribunal set aside a refusal of a Contributory Aged Parent (Residence) (Class DG) Subclass 864 visa and remitted the matter for reconsideration. The key issue was whether the sponsor had been adopted by the applicant before turning 18 through customary arrangements, despite a formal adoption occurring when he was 35. The Tribunal found the pre-18 relationship was in the nature of adoption under the Migration Regulations 1994 (Cth).
Key holding

A pre-18 customary or other adoption-like arrangement outside Australia can satisfy the adoption definition in r 1.04(1)(c) and (2), even if formal adoption occurs later, provided the subreg (2) criteria are met.

The Tribunal therefore is satisfied that the nature of the applicant's and sponsor's relationship while the sponsor was under eighteen were in the nature of adoption under regs 1.04(1)(c) and (2).

2544494 (Refugee)

29 Aug 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2326Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
The Tribunal reviewed a refusal to grant protection visas to an Indian couple and their children who claimed fear of harm from the wife’s family because of the couple’s inter-caste marriage. The delegate and Tribunal both disbelieved the claims, particularly the alleged August 2024 marketplace attack and ongoing threats. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal.

In protection visa credibility assessment, the Tribunal is not obliged to accept an applicant’s allegations uncritically and may reject a factual assertion without having rebutting evidence.

However, I am not required to accept uncritically any or all of the allegations made by an applicant. Further, I am not required to have rebutting evidence available to me before I can find that a particular factual assertion by an applicant has not been made out.

2635010 (Migration)

The Tribunal refused a Bridging E (Class WE) Subclass 050 visa to an Iranian applicant in immigration detention after criminal convictions and prior protection visa refusal. The applicant relied on a pending s 48B ministerial intervention request and asserted he had acceptable arrangements to depart Australia if those avenues failed. The Tribunal affirmed the delegate’s refusal.
Key holding

A Bridging E visa applicant does not satisfy cl 050.212(5B) where they have previously made any s 48B ministerial intervention request, even if the earlier request was finalised as not considered and a later request is treated as non-repeat under the Ministerial Instructions; the Tribunal cannot read the Ministerial Instructions exception into the regulation.

It finds that to conclude for the purposes of cl 050.212(5B) that the applicant's 23 December 2025 request is not a repeat request on the basis of the Ministerial Instructions is to read into cl 050.212(5B) a set of circumstances and provisions that the clause does not contemplate or provide for.

2301755 (Refugee)

24 Jul 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2464Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewVisa integrityMigration
The Tribunal reviewed a delegate's refusal to grant a protection visa to a gay man from East Timor who claimed he would face societal and familial harm, including ostracism, forced marriage and employment discrimination, if returned. Accepting his claims as credible and finding that state protection would not be effective, the Tribunal was satisfied he is a refugee under s 36(2)(a) of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth). The decision was set aside and remitted for reconsideration.
Key holding

An applicant cannot be required to conceal or modify his sexual orientation to avoid persecution, and the modification exceptions in s 5J(3) apply where conformity would conflict with a characteristic fundamental to his identity or conceal an innate or immutable characteristic.

I find that the applicant could not take reasonable steps to modify his behaviour to avoid a real chance of persecution. Such modifications would fall within the various exceptions in s 5J(3), including conflicting with a characteristic that is fundamental to the applicant's identity or conscience and concealing an innate or immutable characteristic.

2209041 (Refugee)

24 Jul 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2443Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
The Tribunal reviewed a refusal of a protection visa to a Timorese applicant who claimed economic hardship, witchcraft-related harm and a land dispute. It accepted he could safely live in Dili and found no well-founded fear of persecution or real risk of significant harm. It affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

A claim based on a localised land dispute fails the refugee criterion where the applicant can live safely elsewhere in the country, because the real chance of persecution does not relate to all areas under s 5J(1)(c).

I find that the applicant does not have a well founded fear of persecution because the real chance of persecution arising from the land dispute does not relate to all areas of Timor Leste: s 5J(1)(c).

2104609 (Refugee)

23 Jul 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2306Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewMigration
In 2104609 (Refugee) [2026] ARTA 2306, the Tribunal reviewed a delegate’s refusal of protection visas to Indian nationals in an interfaith/inter-caste marriage who claimed family and honour-based violence in Punjab. The delegate refused under s 65 because the applicants were not refugees and did not meet complementary protection. The Tribunal set aside the refusal and remitted the matter with a direction that the applicants meet s 36(2)(aa) of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth).
Key holding

A protection visa may be granted on complementary protection grounds where the refugee criterion is not met, if removal creates a real risk of significant harm as a necessary and foreseeable consequence.

The issues in this case are whether the applicants have a well-founded fear of being persecuted in India for one or more of the five reasons set out, and, if not, whether there are substantial grounds for believing that, as a necessary and foreseeable consequence of the applicant being removed from Australia to India there is a real risk that they will suffer significant harm.

Puneetpal Singh (Migration)

22 Jul 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2368Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
The Tribunal reviewed a delegate’s refusal of a Student (Temporary) (Class TU) Subclass 500 visa based on cl 500.214 financial capacity requirements. It accepted the applicant had shown sufficient funds and had paid relevant course fees, but was not satisfied he would have genuine access to funds held by his grandfather. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

Clause 500.214 requires the Tribunal to be satisfied not only that sufficient funds exist but also that the applicant will have genuine access to those funds.

To meet cl 500.214(1), the Tribunal must also be satisfied that the applicant will have genuine access to the funds referred to above.

2017326 (Refugee)

The Tribunal reviewed the refusal of protection visas for a Bangladeshi man and his minor daughter. Key holding

Where a review applicant raises a claim for the first time before the Tribunal and lacks a reasonable explanation for not raising it before the primary decision, the Tribunal is to draw an unfavourable credibility inference under s 367A of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth).

if, in relation to an application for review of a reviewable protection decision, the applicant raises a claim that was not raised before the primary decision was made, in making a decision on the application, the Tribunal is to draw an inference unfavourable to the credibility of the claim if the Tribunal is satisfied that the applicant does not have a reasonable explanation why the claim was not raised before the primary decision was made (s 367A).

2440916 (Refugee)

The assessment of "real risk" of significant harm under complementary protection grounds uses the same test as the "real chance" test which is used when deciding whether there is an objective factual basis for a person's claims for having a well-founded fear of persecution for the purposes of s 36(2)(a) of the Act.

2532892 (Migration)

15 Jul 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2389Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
The Tribunal reviewed a refusal of a Child (Migrant) (Class AH) Subclass 101 visa for an applicant over 18 who was not in a relationship or full-time work and who completed high school in June 2023 but did not start full-time study until December 2023, after the visa application was lodged on 4 August 2023. The Tribunal accepted the study gap was exceptional and beyond his control but held it had no discretion because binding authority requires actual commencement of full-time study by the time of application. It affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

Under cl 101.213(1)(c), a Subclass 101 applicant over 18 must have actually commenced the relevant full-time course of study by the time the visa application is lodged, and the six-month/reasonable-time grace period does not remove that requirement.

The Court held that cl 101.213(1)(c) requires that a visa applicant must have actually commenced a full-time course of study by the time the visa application is lodged and to construe the statutory provision otherwise was to re-write the words 'been undertaking'.

Rushpinder Kaur (Migration)

11 Jul 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2367Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewMigration

Key holding

In assessing the genuine temporary entrant criterion, the Direction No. 108 factors are guides only rather than a checklist, and the decision maker must determine on balance whether the applicant satisfies the criterion having regard to all the circumstances.

The Direction indicates that the factors specified should not be used as a checklist but rather, are intended only to guide decision makers when considering the applicant's circumstances as a whole, in reaching a finding about whether the applicant satisfies the genuine temporary entrant criterion.

Ho (Migration)

3 Jul 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2359Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
The Tribunal reviewed a refusal of a Student (Temporary) (Class TU) Subclass 500 visa on genuine temporary entrant grounds. Although the applicant had completed patisserie and hospitality study in Australia, the Tribunal found inconsistencies in his evidence and strong family and financial ties to Australia through his sister’s patisserie business. It was not satisfied he genuinely intended to stay in Australia temporarily and affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

A student visa applicant fails cl 500.212(a) where the Tribunal, on the whole of the evidence and Direction No 108, is not satisfied that the applicant genuinely intends to stay in Australia temporarily.

As the Tribunal is not satisfied that the applicant intends genuinely to stay in Australia temporarily, the applicant does not meet cl 500.212(a). Having concluded that the applicant does not meet cl 500.212(a) it is not necessary to further consider cl 500.212(b) or (c).

Nisha (Migration)

3 Jul 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2356Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewVisa integrityMigration
Mrs Batulan Nisha applied for a Partner (Temporary) (Class UK) visa (Subclass 820) in December 2018 on the basis of her marriage to Mr John Mitchell. A delegate refused the visa solely because there was no evidence the sponsor was an Australian citizen, permanent resident or eligible New Zealand citizen. On review the applicant produced movement records and citizenship evidence, and the Tribunal, being satisfied the marriage was valid and the relationship genuine and continuing, set aside the refusal and remitted the matter.
Key holding

The absence of joint major assets or liabilities does not tell against the genuineness of a spousal relationship where it is explained by the parties' age and limited income, the reg 1.15A(3) factors being weighed in light of their actual circumstances.

Given their age and limited income, the Tribunal considers the findings that they have no joint major assets or liabilities are not indicative that they are not in a genuine and continuing relationship.

2536325 (Refugee)

1 Jul 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2423Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewVisa integrityMigration
The Tribunal was redetermining a protection visa application remitted by the FCFCOA after the IAA’s nationality finding was quashed. It found the applicant was an Afghan national, not Pakistani, and that as a Hazara/Baloch Shia he faced a real chance of persecution in Afghanistan. The Tribunal set aside the decision and remitted the matter on the basis that the applicant meets s 36(2)(a).
Key holding

Birth in Pakistan does not establish Pakistani nationality for a child of Afghan refugee parents where the evidence shows Pakistani citizenship is systematically denied to Afghans and their descendants; receiving country depends on the law of the relevant country as actually applied.

Accordingly, it seems that being born in Pakistan, which the Applicant readily accepts that he was, does not establish that he is a national of that country. If he was a child born in Pakistan to Afghan refugee parents, for example, the weight of evidence suggests that he would not be a Pakistani citizen.

Teh (Migration)

1 Jul 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2362Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
This case concerns a Subclass 485 (Temporary Graduate) visa applicant who mistakenly selected the Graduate Work stream but would have met the Post-Study Work stream criteria. The Tribunal held it was bound by Obinwa to assess only the stream selected on the visa application form. The delegate’s refusal was affirmed.
Key holding

An applicant who nominates a particular Subclass 485 stream must satisfy the criteria for that stream, and the Tribunal need not consider an alternative stream.

The consequence is that an applicant for a Class VC visa who nominates a particular stream (as every applicant must, in order to make a valid application) must satisfy the criteria applicable to that stream in order for the visa to be granted. Satisfying criteria that are applicable only to another stream does not assist.

Ramirez Garcia (Migration)

1 Jul 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2353Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewMigration
The Tribunal reviewed refusals of Temporary Skill Shortage (Class GK) Subclass 482 visas for a primary applicant nominated as a Marketing Specialist and her family. Although the delegate refused on work-experience grounds, the case was ultimately determined by the sponsor’s cessation of trading and the applicant’s move to a different corporate entity. The Tribunal affirmed the refusals but referred the matter for Ministerial intervention.
Key holding

The genuine intention and genuine position requirements in cl 482.212(2) are not met where the applicant is employed by, and intends to remain with, a different corporate entity from the employer sponsor.

Further, given the primary applicant's current employment with another corporate entity that is not the employer sponsor and the clear evidence that she intends to continue to work at this entity, the Tribunal is not satisfied her intention to perform the nominated occupation and the position associated with the nominated occupation are genuine for the purposes of cl 482.212(2) as applicable to this nomination review.

2317753 (Refugee)

1 Jul 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2323Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration

2407106 (Migration)

1 Jul 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2270Tribunal reviewVisa integrityVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewMigration
The Tribunal reviewed a delegate’s refusal of a Student (Temporary) (Class TU) visa because a bogus bank document had supported the applicant’s earlier Temporary Activity visa. The Tribunal accepted the document was bogus but found the applicant was unaware of it and neither complicit in nor recklessly indifferent to her coach’s or agent’s conduct. It set aside the refusal and remitted the matter with a direction that PIC 4020 is met for cl 500.217(1).
Key holding

For PIC 4020(1), where a third party supplies a bogus document, an applicant may be taken to have caused it to be given if complicit in, or recklessly indifferent to, that third party’s unlawful or dishonest conduct.

If the applicant is complicit in the provision of false information or 'indifferent' to a third party acting unlawfully or dishonestly, it can be said that the applicant caused it to be given.

Sukhdeep Kaur (Migration)

30 Jun 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2352Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewMigration
The Administrative Review Tribunal reviewed a refusal of a Subclass 500 (Student) visa based on the delegate’s finding that Ms Kaur was not a genuine temporary entrant. The Tribunal accepted that her nursing study pathway, course changes, family ties and Indian job offer supported a genuine intention to stay temporarily and remitted the visa application for reconsideration. The decision turns on the application of cl 500.212 and Direction No 108.
Key holding

Changes to course enrolment do not necessarily show that a student visa is being used to maintain residence where the changes are credibly explained and consistent with a genuine study and career intention.

The Tribunal is satisfied that the applicant's intention to study nursing has been consistent, and the changes to course enrolments largely demonstrate the options she undertook in order to achieve her ambition, and were not undertaken for the primary purpose of extending or maintaining residence in Australia.

TUNTIPONGMANEEKUL (Migration)

29 Jun 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2410Tribunal reviewVisa integrityVisa refusal reviewMigration
The applicant sought review of a delegate’s refusal of a Student (Temporary) (Class TU) visa under cl 500.217(1) because her partner visa application disclosed a de facto relationship from 5 January 2021 that had not been declared in her earlier student visa application. The Tribunal found she was in a de facto relationship before the student visa application and that the alleged migration agent error was not established. It affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

PIC 4020 is engaged where there is an element of fraud or deception by some person, although the applicant need not have known that the information was purposely untrue.

While PIC 4020 refers to information that is false, in the sense of purposely untrue, it is not necessary for the Minister (or the Tribunal on review) to conclude that the applicant was aware the information was purposely untrue in order for PIC 4020 to be engaged. However, an element of fraud or deception by some person is necessary to attract the operation of the provision: Trivedi v MIBP [2014] FCAFC 42.

Yip (Migration)

To meet cl 500.214(1), the Tribunal must also be satisfied that the applicant will have genuine access to the funds referred to above.

2412912 (Refugee)

10 Jun 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2469Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
The applicant, a Vietnamese national, initially claimed protection based on general poverty, corruption and lack of opportunity in Vietnam, then raised a new claim at hearing that he feared harm from a loan shark. The Tribunal found the loan-shark claim not credible because it was late-raised, vague, inconsistent and uncorroborated. It affirmed the delegate’s refusal of a protection visa.
Key holding

An applicant bears responsibility under s 5AAA to specify all particulars of the protection claim and provide sufficient evidence; the Tribunal is not required to make the applicant’s case or accept allegations uncritically.

Section 5AAA provides that it is the responsibility of the applicant to specify all particulars of their claim and to provide sufficient evidence to establish the claim. Although the concept of onus of proof is not appropriate to administrative inquiries and decision-making, the relevant facts of the individual case will have to be supplied by the applicant in as much detail as is necessary to enable the decision-maker to establish the relevant facts.

2109695 (Refugee)

5 Jun 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2311Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
The Tribunal (ARTA) affirmed the refusal of a protection visa to a Tongan national who arrived in Australia as a minor and feared homelessness/economic hardship in Tonga due to having no family or support network. It accepted his circumstances but found no entity in Tonga would persecute him or cause significant harm. Ministerial intervention was mentioned but not recommended.
Key holding

Economic hardship or lack of support does not amount to persecution or significant harm unless an entity in the receiving country would cause that harm.

Each of the verbal phrases contained in the categories of harm which form the definition of 'significant harm' contemplate that there is an entity in the country concerned which would cause that harm; that is, 'will be arbitrarily deprived of', 'will be carried out on', and 'will be subjected to'. Because there is no entity which would seek to cause any of those categories of harm to the Applicant, he does not meet the statutory criterion for complementary protection.

2420008 (Refugee)

4 Jun 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2421Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
This is a Tribunal review of a delegate’s refusal of protection visas to a Malaysian husband and wife. The first applicant claimed harm arising from opposition to land acquisition and a proposed chemical factory, but the Tribunal found his claims lacked detail, were inconsistent and were not credible, and affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

Applicants bear responsibility for providing sufficient particulars and supporting evidence, and mere assertions of feared harm do not themselves establish a well-founded fear or real risk of significant harm.

It is well established that the mere assertions by an applicant that he or she fears persecution for particular reasons, such assertions do not of themselves establish the validity of the asserted fears, for the reasons claimed or that such fears are well-founded. Similarly, such claims, that an applicant faces a real risk of significant harm do not of themselves establish that these risks exist or amount to a 'real risk' or significant harm.

14 May 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2461Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration

2303830 (Refugee)

8 May 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2411Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
This was a protection visa review for a Malaysian mother and her child, with the mother claiming fear of her former husband after serious domestic violence in Australia. The Tribunal accepted past family violence and threatening messages but found no objective real chance of future harm in Malaysia and that effective state protection was available. It affirmed both refusal decisions.
Key holding

A subjective fear of persecution is insufficient for refugee status unless the applicant also shows an objective real chance of persecution in the reasonably foreseeable future.

Therefore, while the Tribunal is prepared to accept that the applicant subjectively fears persecution from her ex-husband on return to Malaysia, the Tribunal was not satisfied that the objective requirement was met because 3.5 years has elapsed, the applicant is now divorced from her husband who is without means or connections to track her down and there is no real chance he will cause the applicants any harm in the reasonably foreseeable future.

2006580 (Refugee)

8 May 2026Administrative Review Tribunal[2026] ARTA 2271Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewMigration
The Tribunal reviewed a delegate’s refusal of a subclass 866 protection visa to an Iranian citizen who claims to have converted from Islam to Christianity in Australia. It accepted his core claims, found Iran would not protect him as a Christian convert, set aside the refusal and remitted the matter for reconsideration under s 36(2)(a) of the Migration Act.
Key holding

Where an applicant engages in religious conduct in Australia after arrival, s 5J(6) requires that conduct to be disregarded for the refugee assessment unless the applicant proves it was not done to strengthen the refugee claim; a genuine, sustained conversion supported by evidence can satisfy that requirement.

Section 5J(6) provides that any conduct engaged in by a person in Australia must be disregarded in determining whether the person has a well-founded fear of being persecuted for reasons of race, religion, nationality, or membership of a particular social group or political opinion, unless the person satisfies the decision maker that he or she engaged in the conduct otherwise than for the purpose of strengthening the claim to be a refugee.

EUC19 v Minister for Immigration and Citizenship [2026] FCA 364 (27 March 2026)

27 Mar 2026Federal Court of Australia[2026] FCA 364Migration: court challengeTribunal reviewVisa refusal review
This is an appeal to the Federal Court from a decision of the Federal Circuit Court affirming the Tribunal’s refusal of protection visas. The appellants alleged unfairness, misleading questions, and failure to consider claims. The Federal Court dismissed the appeal, finding no error by the primary judge.
Key holding

On an appeal from a judicial review decision, the appellant bears the onus of establishing error in the primary judge’s reasons; unparticularised allegations of unfairness or misleading questions are insufficient.

The onus fell on the appellants to establish before the primary judge that the conduct of the hearing before the Tribunal, including the translation, gave rise to error.

So v Minister for Immigration and Citizenship [2026] FCA 345 (26 March 2026)

26 Mar 2026Federal Court of Australia[2026] FCA 345Migration: court challengeTribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewCharacter test
The Federal Court reviewed an Administrative Review Tribunal decision affirming non-revocation of the applicant’s mandatorily cancelled Resident Return visa under s 501CA(4) of the Migration Act. The Tribunal found the applicant failed the character test and that there was no another reason to revoke, but the Court held it failed to consider his non-refoulement claims as required by Ministerial Direction 110. The decision was set aside and remitted for redetermination.
Key holding

A decision-maker considering revocation under s 501CA(4) must consider claims capable of engaging international non-refoulement obligations raised by a non-citizen not subject to a protection finding, as required by para 9.1.2(1) of Direction 110.

Paragraph 9.1.2(1) of Direction 110 expressly requires that claims raised by a non-citizen who is not the subject of a protection finding that may give rise to international non-refoulement obligations "must be considered". The provision recognises that a person may raise international non-refoulement obligations distinct from the statutory "protection obligations" assessed in the protection visa process.

Patel v Minister for Immigration and Citizenship [2026] FCA 340 (25 March 2026)

25 Mar 2026Federal Court of Australia[2026] FCA 340Migration: court challengeTribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal review
This was an application to the Federal Court for an extension of time and leave to appeal from an interlocutory FCFCOA decision refusing to reinstate the applicants’ judicial review proceeding. The underlying migration matter concerned refusal of subclass 186 visas because the first applicant had no approved employer nomination. The Court dismissed the application because any appeal and remittal would be futile.
Key holding

Where an applicant cannot satisfy the visa criteria and remittal would inevitably result in the same refusal, it is futile and not in the interests of justice to grant an extension of time and leave to appeal, notwithstanding an arguable jurisdictional error.

Having reviewed the Tribunal's decision, the Court is satisfied that it would be futile for the applicants to be granted an extension of time and leave to appeal because it is not possible for the applicants to meet the requisite criteria for the grant of the visa. The only decision open to the Tribunal was, and would be on remittal, to affirm the delegate's decision to refuse to grant the applicants the visa.

DJC17 v Minister for Immigration and Citizenship [2026] FCA 319 (24 March 2026)

24 Mar 2026Federal Court of Australia[2026] FCA 319Migration: court challengeTribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal review
This was an application to the Federal Court to reinstate an appeal after it was dismissed because the appellants did not appear at the hearing when the matter was called. The appellants said they could not find the courtroom. The Court refused reinstatement because the proposed appeal had no reasonable prospects of success.
Key holding

Reinstatement of an appeal dismissed for non-appearance will be refused where the proposed grounds lack merit and would not warrant a grant of leave at a substantive hearing.

Given the lack of merit in the grounds of appeal, they would not have warranted a grant of leave to appeal in a substantive hearing. The appellants' do not provide a basis on which to reinstate the appeal.

SZRQJ v Minister for Immigration, Citizenship and Multicultural Affairs [2026] FCA 312 (20 March 2026)

20 Mar 2026Federal Court of Australia[2026] FCA 312Migration: court challengeTribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal review
The applicant sought an extension of time to seek leave to appeal from a 2012 Federal Magistrates Court summary judgment dismissing review of a protection visa refusal. She did not appear at the Federal Court hearing despite notice and warnings, and the Court dismissed the extension application for non-appearance with fixed costs.
Key holding

The Court may dismiss an extension application for non-appearance where the applicant had notice, was warned of the consequences, elected not to attend without explanation, and the absence, with failure to file submissions, suggests want of prosecution.

Being satisfied that the applicant had notice of the hearing, was apprised of the potential consequences of the hearing, and elected not to attend the hearing without explanation, I consider that there is no utility in determining her application for an extension of time. Coupled with the failure to provide an outline of written submissions in support of her application, I consider that her absence suggests a want of prosecution of her claim.

ABC18 v Minister for Immigration and Citizenship [2026] FCA 274 (20 March 2026)

20 Mar 2026Federal Court of Australia[2026] FCA 274Migration: court challengeTribunal reviewVisa refusal review
ABC18 appealed to the Federal Court from a Federal Circuit and Family Court of Australia (Div 2) decision dismissing judicial review of an Administrative Appeals Tribunal decision refusing a Protection (Class XA) Subclass 866 visa. The appellant alleged the Tribunal’s adverse credibility findings, based partly on inconsistency between his written and oral protection claims, were illogical, irrational or lacked probative basis. Button J dismissed the appeal with costs.
Key holding

A Tribunal does not act illogically or irrationally by treating a protection applicant’s later oral claims as inconsistent with earlier written claims, and using that inconsistency on credibility, unless it has accepted that the applicant had no knowledge of the earlier written claims.

Unless the Tribunal were to have accepted that the Appellant had no knowledge of the first set of claims being made in his written claims, it was not illogical for the Tribunal to regard the Appellant's accounts as inconsistent and take that into account in assessing the Appellant's oral protection claims.

Sharma v Minister for Immigration and Citizenship [2026] FCA 269 (17 March 2026)

17 Mar 2026Federal Court of Australia[2026] FCA 269Migration: court challengeTribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewMigration
Mr Sharma sought a Regional Sponsored Migration Scheme (subclass 187) visa based on a nomination by Shivram Australia Pty Ltd. The nomination was refused/withdrawn, so the Tribunal affirmed the visa refusal because cl 187.233(3) required an approved nomination, and the Federal Circuit and Family Court dismissed judicial review. On appeal to the Federal Court, Mr Sharma alleged the Tribunal and primary judge failed to engage with his claims that he was defrauded by the nominator and denied natural justice.
Key holding

Third-party fraud does not amount to jurisdictional error unless it stultifies or otherwise affects the decision-maker’s exercise of statutory power; the mandatory approved-nomination criterion remains determinative.

There was no such approved nomination application at the time that the Minister and thereafter the Tribunal considered Mr Sharma's application. Accordingly, whatever the alleged fraud may have been that occurred before the nomination application was refused and the circumstances in which the nominator's review application was withdrawn, that alleged fraud did not affect the Tribunal's, and thereafter the FCFCOA's, power to carry out their respective statutory tasks.

Verbatim excerpt, AI-selected. Check the full text.

HLWS v Minister for Immigration and Multicultural Affairs [2026] FCA 243 (12 March 2026)

12 Mar 2026Federal Court of Australia[2026] FCA 243Migration: court challengeTribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewCharacter test
HLWS sought an extension of time and judicial review of an Administrative Review Tribunal decision affirming refusal of a Resolution of Status visa under s 501(1) of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth). The Tribunal accepted a psychologist’s low/moderate reoffending risk assessed “without intervention” but did not address HLWS’s willingness to undertake interventions. The Federal Court granted the extension and allowed review, finding a material jurisdictional error.
Key holding

A tribunal commits jurisdictional error where it overlooks a substantial and clearly articulated contention that is material to its statutory risk assessment, such that the outcome could realistically have been different.

Accordingly, in my view, in failing to consider the interventions point at all, the Tribunal failed to discharge the task and function accorded to it by Parliament, and engaged in jurisdictional error.

San Bao Pty Ltd v Minister for Immigration and Citizenship [2026] HCA 6 (11 March 2026)

11 Mar 2026High Court of Australia[2026] HCA 6Migration: court challengeTribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal review
San Bao Pty Ltd sought constitutional writs of certiorari and mandamus in the High Court after a delegate refused its nomination of Ms Haiming Du for a Subclass 482 (Skills in Demand) visa, the Company having missed the 28-day ART review period. The Company alleged the delegate constructively failed to exercise jurisdiction or acted unreasonably by refusing the nomination on the supposed basis that no organisational chart or staffing-structure evidence had been provided.
Key holding

A decision-maker’s reasons must be read as a whole and in context; a reference to an absent organisational chart does not establish that the decision-maker mistakenly believed no chart was provided where the actual concern was insufficient evidence of how the nominated role fitted the staffing structure.

When the references by the delegate in their reasons to an "organisation[al] chart" are properly understood in their context, it can be seen that the delegate was concerned with the absence of sufficient evidence, including from an organisational chart, that explained the need for an additional cook by detailing the relationship that the additional cook would have within the existing structure.

Michales v CharterLaw Legal Pty Limited [2026] FCA 209 (6 March 2026)

6 Mar 2026Federal Court of Australia[2026] FCA 209Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal review
Mr Michales sought review of a Registrar’s decision refusing to set aside bankruptcy notice BN279414, issued on CharterLaw Legal Pty Ltd’s application for unpaid legal fees totalling $107,654.53. He argued CLL could not recover or enforce the debt because it had ceased legal practice on 30 June 2024 and was not a qualified entity under the Legal Profession Uniform Law (NSW). Perry J dismissed the application, refused amendment/subpoena/notice to produce relief, and upheld the bankruptcy notice.
Key holding

A company that provided legal services while licensed does not contravene s 10(1) of the LPUL merely by suing on its own behalf to recover unpaid fees after ceasing practice.

By commencing proceedings on its own behalf to recover a debt from the applicant, CLL does not engage in legal practice.

2114521 (Refugee)

The Tribunal reviewed a delegate’s refusal of a subclass 866 protection visa to a Chinese national who claimed persecution arising from a family shop/land dispute involving local government, a developer and his father’s petitioning. At hearing, the applicant contradicted those written claims, said he did not fear harm on return to China, and explained he wished to stay in Australia for work and family financial reasons. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

An applicant for a protection visa bears the responsibility to establish all statutory elements by sufficient evidence, and the Tribunal is not required to make the applicant’s case.

It remains for the applicant to satisfy the Tribunal that all of the statutory elements are established. A decision-maker is not required to make the applicant's case for him or her.
