October 2024 (101)

2002567 (Refugee)

11 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4218Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Don Smyth
This was an AATA review of a delegate’s refusal of a protection visa to an applicant who claimed South African citizenship but was born and lived in Zimbabwe. He claimed fear in South Africa and/or Zimbabwe as a gay, mixed-race/“coloured” man, a political opponent of Zanu-PF, and because of economic conditions and crime. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

For complementary protection, a real risk is not established where the risk is one faced by the population generally and not personally by the applicant.

Under s 36(2B)(c) of the Act there is taken not to be a real risk that an applicant will suffer significant harm if the Tribunal is satisfied that the real risk is one faced by the population generally and is not faced by the applicant personally.

2016849 (Refugee)

11 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4209Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Lilly Mojsin
This was an Administrative Review Tribunal (Migration & Refugee Division) review of a delegate’s refusal to grant protection visas to Chinese applicants who claimed harm from loan sharks, debt collectors, gangdom and police in China. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal because the applicants consented to a decision on the papers and the Tribunal was not satisfied their claims were made out or that they faced a real chance or real risk of harm on return to China.
Key holding

A protection visa applicant bears the onus of establishing all statutory elements; mere assertions of feared persecution or significant harm are insufficient unless they satisfy the Tribunal that a well-founded fear or real risk exists.

The mere fact that a person claims fear of persecution for a particular reason does not establish either the genuineness of the asserted fear or that it is "well-founded" or that it is for the reason claimed. Similarly, that the applicant claims to face a real risk of significant harm does not establish that such a risk exists, or that the harm feared amounts to "significant harm".

1919845 (Refugee)

10 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4472Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Rupert Timms
The Tribunal reviewed a delegate’s refusal of a protection visa to a Vietnamese Kinh Buddhist man who claimed fear of harm in Vietnam arising from low-level protest activity in Australia. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal because the evidence did not establish a well-founded fear of persecution or a real risk of significant harm. It also found no jurisdiction over the son’s review because the son had become an Australian citizen, but referred the father’s case to the Minister under s 417.
Key holding

Conduct engaged in by a protection visa applicant in Australia must be disregarded in assessing well-founded fear unless the applicant proves it was not done to strengthen the refugee claim.

In determining whether the person has a well‑founded fear of persecution for one or more of the reasons mentioned in paragraph (1)(a), any conduct engaged in by the person in Australia is to be disregarded unless the person satisfies the Minister that the person engaged in the conduct otherwise than for the purpose of strengthening the person's claim to be a refugee.

1917587 (Refugee)

10 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4461Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Frank Russo
The Tribunal reviewed a refusal of protection visas for a Thai family who claimed they feared harm in Thailand from a money lender associated with organised crime over unpaid agricultural debts. The Tribunal accepted the applicants’ identity and nationality but made adverse credibility findings and found no well-founded fear of persecution or real risk of significant harm. It affirmed the delegate’s refusal.
Key holding

A claimed fear of persecution is not well-founded unless supported by credible, non-speculative evidence; the Tribunal may reject uncritical assertions and find the claimed fear not established.

The Tribunal notes that the mere fact that a person claims fear of persecution for a particular reason does not establish the genuineness of the asserted fear, that the fear is "well-founded" or that it is for the reason claimed. A fear of persecution is not "well-founded" if it is merely assumed or if it is mere speculation.

1917784 (Refugee)

10 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4434Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Mary-Ann Cooper
The Tribunal reviewed a delegate’s refusal of a protection visa to a Chinese citizen who claimed he would be harmed in China by loan sharks over an unpaid debt. The Tribunal disbelieved the loan-shark claims, accepted only that he owed money to relatives, and found neither the refugee nor complementary protection criteria were met. It affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

Harm arising merely from failure to repay a debt is not persecution for a Convention reason under s 5J(1)(a), so it cannot found refugee status.

This is the essential and significant reason for the harm the applicant claims he will suffer, and is not one of the 5 reasons listed in s 5J(1) of the Act.

2421655 (Refugee)

10 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4235Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Adrienne Anderson
The applicant sought review of a delegate’s refusal of a protection visa (China). The Tribunal found it had no jurisdiction because the review application was lodged after the statutory 28-day period, having been validly notified by email to the address last provided for receiving documents. The Tribunal could not extend time.
Key holding

The Tribunal lacks jurisdiction over a protection visa review application lodged after the statutory period where the applicant was validly notified and there is no power to extend time.

The Tribunal has no discretion to extend the time period for making a valid review application. As the application for review was not received by the Tribunal until 7 July 2024 the application for review was not made in accordance with the relevant legislation and the Tribunal has no jurisdiction in this matter.

2015178 (Refugee)

10 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4170Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Khanh Hoang
The applicant, an Indonesian citizen, sought review of a delegate’s refusal of a protection visa based on claims she was threatened by an illegal money-lending boss after her boyfriend allegedly took USD 10,000. The Tribunal proceeded without a hearing because she did not attend after a valid invitation, found her claims lacked sufficient detail, and affirmed the refusal. It found she did not meet either the refugee or complementary protection criteria.
Key holding

In a protection visa review, the applicant bears the responsibility to specify all particulars and provide sufficient evidence, and the Tribunal is not obliged to make the applicant’s case.

It is the responsibility of the applicant to specify all particulars of the claim to be a person in respect of whom Australia has protection obligations and to provide sufficient evidence to establish the claim. The Tribunal does not have any responsibility or obligation to specify, or assist in specifying any particulars of the claim, or to establish or assist in establishing the claim: s 5AAA.

1904506 (Refugee)

10 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4070Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Paul White
This is an Administrative Appeals Tribunal review of a delegate’s refusal to grant a protection visa to a Ghanaian citizen who claimed fear of harm in Ghana based on alleged trade union activism and his involvement in the 2018 Commonwealth Games visa scandal. The Tribunal rejected both claims, finding the trade union claim was raised late and not credible, and that country information did not establish a real chance or risk of serious or significant harm arising from the visa scandal. It affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

Where an applicant raises a new claim or evidence before the Tribunal that was not raised before the delegate, s 423A requires the Tribunal to draw an unfavourable credibility inference if the applicant has no reasonable explanation for the delay.

In circumstances where the applicants have raised a claim or presented evidence that was not first raised or presented before the delegate's decision, s 423A of the Act requires the Tribunal to draw an inference unfavourable to the credibility of that claim or evidence if the Tribunal is satisfied that the applicant does not have a reasonable explanation why the claim was not raised, or the evidence was not presented, before the primary decision made by the delegate.

Sukhwinder Singh (Migration)

10 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4024Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewMigration

Mr Sukhwinder Singh applied for a Subclass 494 Skilled Employer Sponsored Regional (Provisional) visa based on a nomination by Lawson Chinese Pty Ltd. The delegate refused the visa because there was no approved nomination, and the sponsor later withdrew its review of the nomination refusal. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal because the applicant could not satisfy the mandatory approved-nomination criterion.
Key holding

For a Subclass 494 visa, the mandatory cl 494.213 criterion is not met where the nomination identified in the application has not been approved under s 140GB, so the refusal must be affirmed.

Lea (Migration)

10 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4022Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: P. Maishman
Mr Lea applied for a Bridging E (Class WE) visa on 27 September 2024; a delegate refused it on 2 October 2024. On review, the Tribunal affirmed because he did not meet cl 050.212, specifically cl 050.212(3). It found he had no valid substantive visa application and was not satisfied he would apply for one within a specified period.
Key holding

To satisfy cl 050.212(3), a Bridging E applicant must have made a valid substantive visa application in Australia that remains undetermined, or satisfy the Tribunal that they would apply for such a visa within a specified period.

Subclause 050.212(3) is met if the applicant has made, in Australia, a valid application for a substantive visa of a kind that can be granted if the applicant is in Australia and that application has not been finally determined, or the Tribunal is satisfied that the applicant would apply for such a visa within a period specified for doing so.

Khamatham (Migration)

10 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4015Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Christine Kannis
The Tribunal reviewed the cancellation of Mr Khamatham’s Subclass 500 (Student) (Class TU) visa for breaching condition 8202(2)(a) because he was not enrolled in a full-time registered course from 11 August 2022 to 10 July 2023. The Tribunal found the breach established and, after considering discretionary factors, affirmed the cancellation decision.
Key holding

Condition 8202(2)(a) requires a student visa holder to remain enrolled in a full-time registered course, and ceasing enrolment breaches that condition.

The requirements of condition 8202 do not allow the visa holder to cease to be enrolled in a course: Liu v MIMIA [2003] FCA 1170.

BIERNACKI (Migration)

9 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4481Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Wendy Banfield
This Tribunal review concerns a refusal of a Student (Temporary) (Class TU) visa because the applicant did not provide a current Polish police clearance certificate required under reg 2.03AA(2)(a) of the Migration Regulations 1994 (Cth). The applicant claimed delays and difficulties in obtaining the certificate from Poland, but the Tribunal found it was reasonable for him to obtain it and refused to waive the requirement. The Tribunal affirmed the visa refusal.
Key holding

Where a current overseas police clearance is requested under reg 2.03AA(2)(a), the Tribunal will not waive that requirement unless satisfied it is not reasonable for the applicant to provide the statement, and an applicant’s failure to pursue available application procedures means the waiver does not apply.

The Tribunal is not satisfied that it is not reasonable for the applicant to provide the requested statement from an approved authority. The waiver in reg 2.03AA(3) does not apply.

2312268 (Refugee)

9 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4450Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Dr Greg Weeks
The AATA (Migration & Refugee Division) reviewed a refusal of a protection visa to a Czech citizen who had lived in Australia since 1997, was homeless for 20 years, and has serious physical/cognitive health issues and cancer. The Tribunal accepted his circumstances were compassionate but found he did not meet the refugee or complementary protection criteria. It affirmed the visa refusal and referred his case to the Minister under s 417 for possible compassionate intervention.
Key holding

For complementary protection, significant harm under the Migration Act requires harm intentionally inflicted by another party; social or health hardship from lack of support is insufficient.

The definition of significant harm under the complementary protection criterion in ss 5(1) and 36(2A) of the Act is exhaustive. Each of its elements require that harm be intentionally inflicted on an applicant by another party.

Bury (Migration)

9 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4040Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Brygyda Maiden
The Tribunal reviewed the refusal of a Partner (Temporary) (Class UK) Subclass 820 visa to Mr Bury, a British citizen, based on his claimed de facto relationship with his Australian sponsor. Although the Tribunal accepted the relationship at time of application and found the Queensland registered relationship removed the 12-month de facto requirement, it found no current evidence from 2022 onward that the relationship continued at time of decision. It affirmed the visa refusal.
Key holding

For a Subclass 820 partner visa, the de facto relationship requirements in s 5CB(2) must continue to be met at the time of decision, and historic evidence alone may be insufficient where current evidence is lacking.

However, the Tribunal is not satisfied that the requirements of s 5CB(2) continue to be met at the time of decision (in particular that the parties have a mutual commitment to a shared life as required by s 5CB(2)(a) or that the relationship between them is genuine and continuing as required by s 5CB(2)(b).

Jaafouri (Migration)

9 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4036Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Donna Petrovich
This is a Tribunal review of a refused Partner (Migrant) (Class BC) visa where the applicant claimed to have suffered family violence from his sponsor after the relationship ended. The Tribunal accepted the relationship existed and had ceased, but found the non-judicial family violence claim was not established and an Independent Expert opined that relevant family violence did not occur. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

Where a non-judicial family violence claim is not accepted and a properly made Independent Expert opinion finds no relevant family violence, the Tribunal must treat that opinion as correct and cannot find the applicant suffered family violence.

Under reg 1.23 the Tribunal is required to take as correct an Independent Expert's opinion, properly made. Accordingly, the Tribunal finds that the applicant is not taken to have suffered family violence committed by the sponsor for reg 1.22.

Mujibullah (Migration)

9 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4013Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Kira Raif
The Tribunal affirmed refusal of a Child (Migrant) (Class AH) Subclass 101 visa for a stateless applicant over 18 living in a Bangladesh refugee camp. She had not undertaken formal full-time study since Year 4, and the Tribunal rejected the claim that she was incapacitated for work at the time of application. The case turned on the Subclass 101 study and incapacity criteria.
Key holding

For a Subclass 101 Child visa applicant over 18, the cl 101.213(1)(c) study requirement is not excused by reasons for not studying; the applicant must have commenced a full-time course of study by the time of visa application.

However, the Court confirmed that, regardless of the reasons for not commencing study since completing school, or turning 18, an applicant must have commenced a full-time course of study at the time the visa application is made.

Key holding

A Subclass 050 Bridging E (Class WE) visa applicant must meet at least one of the alternate grounds in cl 050.212(2)-(9) at the time of application and continue to satisfy that ground at the time of decision under cl 050.221.

At the time of the visa application, the applicant must meet one of the alternatives set out in cl 050.212(2)-(9). The applicant must continue to satisfy this criterion at the time of decision: cl 050.221.

Sadh (Migration)

8 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4051Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Christine Cody
The Tribunal considered an application to review a delegate’s refusal of a Visitor (Class FA) (Subclass 600) tourist stream visa. The visa applicant had applied offshore and there was no relevant sponsorship, nomination or included relative particulars. The Tribunal found the refusal was not a reviewable decision and it had no jurisdiction.
Key holding

The Tribunal lacks jurisdiction to review a visitor visa refusal where the decision does not satisfy any reviewable-decision category under the Migration Act, including because the applicant was offshore and no relevant sponsorship, nomination or relative particulars existed.

As the delegate's decision is not reviewable under Parts 5 or 7 of the Act it follows that the application for review was not properly made and the Tribunal does not have jurisdiction in this matter.

1914255 (Refugee)

8 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4480Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Mark Oakman
This Tribunal review concerned a Sri Lankan Tamil Hindu applicant who sought a protection visa, relying on his father’s low-level LTTE support, his Tamil race and Hindu religion, and fears arising from being a returned failed asylum seeker who had lived in India and Australia. The Tribunal affirmed the delegate’s refusal, finding that none of his claimed fears were well-founded. The case turned largely on country information, credibility, and the applicant’s low profile.
Key holding

In the particular circumstances of a low-profile Tamil applicant, monitoring and surveillance by Sri Lankan authorities does not amount to serious harm for the purposes of s 5J.

In the particular circumstances of this case, and noting that DFAT reports Tamils who are monitored face harassment through official visits and questioning, but does not otherwise suggest they face any other harm, and also taking into account any general anxiety the applicant may suffer as a result, I do not consider that such monitoring and surveillance would amount to serious harm for the purposes of s 5J of the Act.

1926687 (Refugee)

8 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4479Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Suseela Durvasula
The Tribunal affirmed refusal of a protection visa to an elderly Lebanese Sunni Muslim woman who claimed past family abuse and feared returning due to inadequate medical care, Lebanon’s economic crisis and security situation. The Tribunal rejected the past abuse claims, accepted a real chance of serious harm from inadequate healthcare but found it was not for a Convention reason and not intentionally inflicted. The matter was referred to the Minister for compassionate consideration.
Key holding

A complementary protection claim fails where the real risk of significant harm is one faced by the population generally rather than by the applicant personally.

Under s 36(2B)(c) of the Act, there is taken not to be a real risk that an applicant will suffer significant harm if the Tribunal is satisfied that the real risk is one faced by the population generally and is not faced by the applicant personally.

2406638 (Refugee)

8 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4476Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Clyde Cosentino
The Tribunal affirmed refusal of a protection visa to a Papua New Guinea citizen who claimed fear of tribal violence in Enga Province. It found his claims about conflict, killed relatives and missing parents not credible, partly because his oral evidence showed his parents and siblings were living safely in Town 1. It also found his desire to stay in Australia to support family was economic and did not engage protection obligations.
Key holding

General economic conditions causing disadvantage do not amount to persecution unless the discrimination is for a Convention reason.

The Tribunal finds that general economic conditions in Papua New Guinea which result in economic disadvantage, does not amount to persecution. While persecution may take a variety of forms of social, political and economic discrimination, persecution must involve discrimination against a person, whether individually or as a member of a group, because of race, religion, nationality, political opinion or membership of a particular social group.

2107032 (Refugee)

8 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4471Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Alexander Reilly
The applicant, a Nepalese Hindu, sought protection fearing honour killing by his former Muslim girlfriend’s family after an interfaith relationship in Nepal. The Tribunal accepted past assault, harassment, kidnapping and threats, but found no real chance of future harm because the relationship ended over 11 years earlier and there had been no contact. It affirmed the delegate’s refusal, also finding the applicant had a right to enter and reside in India under the Nepal-India Treaty and had not taken all possible steps to avail himself of it.
Key holding

A protection claim requires a real chance, not a remote or far-fetched possibility, of serious harm in the reasonably foreseeable future.

Under s 5J(1)(b), there must be a real chance that the applicant will suffer serious harm in the reasonably foreseeable future. A real chance is a substantial chance, as distinct from a remote or far-fetched possibility.

1910741 (Refugee)

8 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4470Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Noelle Hossen
The Tribunal affirmed refusal of a protection visa to a Malaysian citizen who claimed he would be pursued and harmed by an illegal money lender/loan shark after his Malaysian business failed and his family borrowed money. The applicant did not attend the Tribunal hearing or provide further evidence, and the Tribunal found no real chance of serious harm or real risk of significant harm on return.
Key holding

For complementary protection, the “real risk” of significant harm is assessed by the same standard as the “real chance” of serious harm for refugee persecution.

The real risk test imposes the same standard as the real chance test applicable to the assessment of well-founded fear. MIAC v SZQRB (2013) FCAFC 33.

1917702 (Refugee)

8 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4443Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Rachelle Johnston
The Tribunal reviewed a refusal of a protection visa to a Chinese citizen who claimed a fear of persecution in China because of his Christian faith. The applicant elected not to attend a hearing and consented to a decision on the papers. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal because the claims lacked sufficient detail and evidence to establish refugee or complementary protection criteria.
Key holding

An applicant for a protection visa bears the responsibility to specify all particulars and provide sufficient evidence to establish their protection claim, and the Tribunal is not obliged to assist in specifying or establishing that claim.

It is the responsibility of an applicant for a protection visa to specify all particulars of his or her claim to be owed protection and to provide sufficient evidence to establish the claim: s 5AAA(2) of the Act. The Tribunal does not have any responsibility or obligation to specify or assist in specifying any particulars of the applicant's claim, or to establish or assist in establishing the claim: s 5AAA(4).

2014646 (Refugee)

8 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4264Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Senior Member G.A.F. Connolly
The applicant, a Chinese citizen, sought review of a delegate’s refusal to grant a protection visa based on claims of forced eviction, protest, detention and persecution in China. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal because the applicant provided no further evidence or particulars over seven years and did not answer the Department’s s 56 request. The Tribunal found no well-founded fear of persecution or real risk of significant harm.
Key holding

A protection visa applicant must supply the relevant facts in sufficient detail for the decision-maker to establish them, and the Tribunal is not required to make the applicant’s case.

Although the concept of onus of proof is inappropriate to administrative inquiries and decision-making of the kind done by this Tribunal, the relevant facts of the individual case will have to be supplied by the applicant in as much detail as is necessary to enable the decision-maker to establish the relevant facts. A decision-maker is not required to make the applicant's case for them.

2318406 (Refugee)

8 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4242Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Paul Millar
The Tribunal considered a second application for review of the same delegate’s refusal of a protection visa. Because the delegate’s decision had already been reviewed and determined by the Tribunal, the Tribunal found it had no jurisdiction to review the matter again.
Key holding

Once a delegate’s decision has been validly reviewed by the Tribunal, it ceases to be a reviewable decision and the Tribunal cannot review it a second time.

The Tribunal has no jurisdiction to review a delegate's decision twice: Jayasinghe v MIEA (1997) 76 FCR 301 and SZASP v MIAC [2007] FCA 771.

Kamara and Minister for Immigration, Citizenship and Multicultural Affairs (Citizenship)

8 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3560Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
The Applicant sought review of a delegate’s decision refusing his citizenship by conferral application because the delegate was not satisfied he was of good character under s 21(2)(h) of the Australian Citizenship Act 2007 (Cth). The Tribunal found the Applicant was of good character despite historical offending, a 2016 drink-driving offence, traffic infringements and disclosure issues. It remitted the matter with a direction that he satisfies s 21(2)(h).
Key holding

The good character criterion in s 21(2)(h) requires a broad moral assessment against the moral norms of the Australian community, rather than a purely conviction-based test.

The character test is ultimately a moral test, and morality can only be tested within the confines of moral norms in society.

SRFX and Minister for Immigration, Citizenship and Multicultural Affairs (Citizenship)

8 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3549Tribunal reviewVisa integrityVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
SRFX applied for Australian citizenship by conferral, but a delegate refused approval under s 24(1) of the Australian Citizenship Act 2007 (Cth), finding he did not satisfy the good character requirement in s 21(2)(h) and that his identity could not be established under s 24(3). The Tribunal reviewed the decision, relying on the applicant’s migration history, prior partner visa findings and allegedly bogus documents, and affirmed the refusal. The case turns on whether the Tribunal could be satisfied of his good character and identity.
Key holding

In assessing good character for citizenship, the decision-maker must make a holistic assessment of the person’s enduring moral qualities, and a single sufficiently serious adverse incident can outweigh otherwise good qualities.

The decision-maker must look holistically at applicant's behaviour over time and reach a conclusion about the person's enduring moral qualities. Notwithstanding the obligation to take a holistic approach, it is established that a single adverse incident of sufficient weight and seriousness can outweigh many good qualities in a person.

The Tribunal will not extend time under s 29(7) of the AAT Act where it is not satisfied that an extension is reasonable in all the circumstances, including because the substantive application has no prospect of success.

The Tribunal declines to extend time under s 29(7) of the Administrative Appeals Tribunal Act 1975 because it is not satisfied that it is reasonable in all the circumstances so to do. The substantive application has no prospect of success.

1813018 (Refugee)

7 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4447Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Mary-Ann Cooper
The applicant, an Indonesian citizen, sought a protection visa claiming he was threatened by a government officer after refusing to inflate prices and would suffer financial hardship on return. The Tribunal accepted some claims at face value but found no Convention-related persecution and no real risk of significant harm. It affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

Financial hardship arising from general economic conditions or re-establishment after return does not amount to significant harm under s 36(2A) because it lacks intentional infliction of pain or suffering.

The Tribunal is not satisfied that any financial hardship the applicant might suffer on his return to Indonesia constitutes cruel or inhuman treatment or punishment or degrading treatment or punishment. Such financial hardship, while it may cause stress and anxiety, is not the result of intentionally inflicted pain or suffering by any person or group.

2410235 (Migration)

7 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4272Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Michael Ison
The Tribunal affirmed cancellation of an Albanian national’s Subclass 010 (Bridging A) visa under s 116(1)(g) and reg 2.43(1)(oa) because he had convictions for cannabis cultivation in 2018 and 2021. Although cancellation would cause family and psychological hardship, the Tribunal gave determinative weight to adverse credibility findings about his explanations for the offending and the absence of extenuating circumstances. He continued to hold a Bridging E visa permitting work, so cancellation did not require departure.
Key holding

Where a prescribed cancellation ground is established but cancellation is not mandatory, the Tribunal must still determine whether the visa should be cancelled in the exercise of its discretion.

For these reasons, the Tribunal is satisfied that the ground for cancellation in s 116(1)(g) and reg 2.43(1)(oa) exists. As that ground does not require mandatory cancellation under s 116(3), the Tribunal must proceed to consider whether the visa should be cancelled.

1907845 (Refugee)

7 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4059Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Ben Goulding
The applicant, a Malaysian citizen, sought a Protection (Class XA) Subclass 866 visa claiming he is bisexual and feared imprisonment, whipping and discrimination in Malaysia under the Penal Code and Sharia law. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal because the applicant gave only general country-level claims and insufficient personal evidence, so it could not be satisfied he was bisexual or faced a real chance/real risk of harm.
Key holding

A protection visa applicant bears the responsibility to provide sufficient evidence to establish each statutory element; merely claiming a fear or risk does not establish the claim.

The mere fact that a person claims fear of persecution for a particular reason does not establish either the genuineness of the asserted fear or that it is 'well-founded' or that it is for the reason claimed. Similarly, that an applicant claims to face a real risk of significant harm does not establish that such a risk exists, or that the harm feared amounts to 'significant harm'.

Melville (Migration)

7 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4042Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Christine Kannis
Mrs Melville applied for a Subclass 461 visa nine days after her substantive visa expired. The Tribunal affirmed refusal because she did not satisfy cl 461.213 and Schedule 3 criterion 3004(c), as her late lodgement was within her control. Although the Tribunal found compelling reasons under 3004(d) and referred the matter to the Minister under s 351, it had no waiver power.
Key holding

To satisfy Schedule 3 criterion 3004(c), the applicant must show that the lack of a substantive visa was caused by factors beyond their control; a failure to check eligibility and lodge before expiry is not sufficient.

In the Tribunal's view the onus is on the visa applicant to check their eligibility before applying and to ensure that they lodge their application prior to the expiry of their visa.

Devi (Migration)

7 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4008Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Alison Murphy
Ms Devi sought review of a refusal to grant an Other Family (Residence) (Class BU) visa, specifically a Subclass 836 (Carer) visa. Her Carer Visa Assessment Certificate recorded an impairment rating of 10, below the required rating of 30. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

Under reg 1.15AA(1)(c), a carer visa applicant must provide a certificate specifying an impairment rating equal to or exceeding the Minister-specified threshold of 30.

Regulation 1.15AA(1)(c) provides that the impairment rating specified in the certificate must be equal to, or exceed, the impairment rating specified by the Minister in a legislative instrument. The relevant instrument for these purposes is IMMI 07/012 and it specifies that the impairment rating for the purposes of reg 1.15AA(1)(c) is 30.

2018339 (Refugee)

6 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4445Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Wayne Pennell
The Tribunal reviewed a delegate’s refusal of a protection visa to a Vietnamese citizen who claimed fear of persecution as a Catholic and as an active member of Viet Tan in Australia. It accepted she had an actual, ongoing and publicly identifiable political profile through Viet Tan and online anti-government activity. The matter was remitted with a direction that she satisfies s 36(2)(a) of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth).
Key holding

Where an applicant has an actual, ongoing and publicly identifiable political profile—such as active Viet Tan membership and online anti-government commentary—and country information shows a real chance of persecution on return for political opinion, the applicant has a well-founded fear under s 5J and is a refugee under s 5H.

Having considered all the circumstances as they apply individually and cumulatively to the applicant, the Tribunal finds that there is a real chance she will be persecuted for reasons of her political opinion if she were to return to Vietnam, and her fear of persecution is well-founded as required by section 5J of the Act.

LIN (Migration)

6 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4056Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: T. Quinn
Miss Xinna Lin sought review of a delegate’s refusal of a Subclass 500 (Student) visa because she had not provided English language proficiency evidence. The Tribunal found she was exempt under IMMI 18/015 because she had completed a substantial component of her Diploma of Information Technology in Australia in English while holding a student visa in the two years before applying. It remitted the visa application for reconsideration with a direction that cl 500.213 is met.
Key holding

Under IMMI 18/015, a Subclass 500 visa applicant satisfies the English language requirement in cl 500.213(1) if, in the two years before applying, they successfully completed a substantial component of an AQF Certificate IV or higher course conducted in Australia in English while holding a student visa.

The relevant instrument IMMI 18/015 indicates that an applicant, who in the two years before applying for a Subclass 500 (Student) visa, has successfully completed a substantial component of a course leading to a qualification from the Australian Qualifications Framework at the Certificate IV level or higher that was conducted in Australia and in English, while the applicant was holding a student visa meets the English language requirements of clause 500.213(1).

The Tribunal found it had no jurisdiction because the application was out of time.

Therefore the prescribed period to apply for review ended on 30 July 2024. As the application for review was not received by the Tribunal until 6 August 2024 the application for review was not made in accordance with the relevant legislation and the Tribunal has no jurisdiction in this matter.

1901651 (Refugee)

4 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4464Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration

The Tribunal affirmed the refusal of a protection visa to a Malaysian citizen who abandoned his original gang-related claims and instead relied on loan-shark threats, financial hardship, discrimination as an Indian Malay, and Catholicism. Not all discrimination or harm will meet the statutory thresholds for serious harm under s 5J(5) or significant harm under s 36(2A).

However, as discussed with the applicant at hearing, not all discrimination or harm will rise to the level of serious harm for the refugee assessment or significant harm for the complementary protection assessment.

1913348 (Refugee)

4 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4453Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Bryn Butler
The Tribunal affirmed refusal of a protection visa to a Pakistani citizen who claimed to have converted from Sunni to Shia Islam in Australia and feared harm from family, community and extremists if returned. Although he claimed a 2017 attack and a business dispute with a childhood friend, the Tribunal found the real chance of persecution did not relate to all areas of Pakistan and relocation was reasonable. Decision: 1913348 (Refugee) [2024] AATA 4453, 4 October 2024.
Key holding

For the refugee criterion, a person has a well-founded fear only if the real chance of persecution relates to all areas of the relevant country.

Under s 5J(1), a person has a well-founded fear of persecution if they fear being persecuted for reasons of race, religion, nationality, membership of a particular social group or political opinion, there is a real chance they would be persecuted for one or more of those reasons, and the real chance of persecution relates to all areas of the relevant country.

2320261 (Refugee)

4 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4442Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Jessica Edis
The Tribunal affirmed refusal of a protection visa to a Zambian man who claimed he feared harm because he and his family suspected his father, a former senior Zambian official, was poisoned and that associates died suspiciously. The Tribunal accepted the applicant was sincere but found his claims speculative and not Convention-related, with no real risk of significant harm. It nevertheless recommended Ministerial intervention under s 417 due to strong compassionate circumstances affecting his Australian wife and child.
Key holding

For the refugee criterion, an applicant must show the feared persecution is linked to a s 5J(1)(a) Convention reason; a uniquely personal fear without that nexus cannot satisfy s 36(2)(a).

He has never suggested (nor is there any evidence which suggests) that his fear is linked to any one or more of the reasons set out in s 5J(1)(a).

Kusmi (Migration)

4 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4058Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Richard Guemy
The applicant sought review of a delegate's refusal of a Student (Temporary) (Class TU) visa. The Tribunal found the review application was lodged out of time and that it had no jurisdiction. The key dispute was whether the refusal notification complied with the statutory notification requirements for an authorised recipient.
Key holding

A review application must be made within 21 days after notification of the refusal, and the Tribunal has no power to extend that statutory period.

Pursuant to s 347(1)(b) of the Act and reg 4.10 of the Migration Regulations 1994 (Cth) (the Regulations) an application for review of this decision had to be made within 21 days after the applicant was notified of the decision in accordance with the statutory requirements. This period in which to make a review application is prescribed by law and cannot be extended by the Tribunal.

Unfortunately for the applicant, these matters are not relevant to the question of whether the Tribunal has jurisdiction to review this decision, because the legislation requires the application to be made within the specified timeframe and does not provide any discretion to extend the time for applying for review of a decision of this kind.

The applicant, a Malaysian citizen, sought review of a protection visa refusal, claiming economic hardship and inability to find work/support family if returned to Malaysia. The decision turned on the absence of a Convention reason and insufficient evidence of real risk of significant harm.
Key holding

A protection visa applicant must establish that any well-founded fear of persecution is for a Convention reason; claims that do not fall within or meet a Convention reason cannot satisfy the refugee criterion.

Based upon the material submitted to support his application and the evidence given at the hearing, the Tribunal finds there to be insufficient evidence to substantiate the applicant has a well-founded fear of persecution for reasons of race, religion, nationality, membership of a particular social group or political opinion, as the claims made by the applicant do not fall within or meet any of those reasons, nor does it demonstrate a well-founded fear of persecution.

2428893 (Refugee)

Key holding

For complementary protection, a real risk of significant harm is assessed on the same standard as a real chance of persecution for refugee status.

The real risk test imposes the same standard as the real chance test applicable to the assessment of a well-founded fear of persecution.

2009058 (Refugee)

3 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4435Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewVisa integrityMigration
Before: Mark O'Loughlin
The Tribunal affirmed the refusal of a protection visa to a Thai applicant who claimed Red Shirt political activism, past arrest/questioning, and fear of arrest or killing if returned to Thailand. The applicant did not attend the hearing and consented to a decision on the papers, providing no further evidence. The Tribunal did not accept her claims as credible or well-founded.
Key holding

Under s 5AAA, an applicant bears the responsibility to specify all particulars of a protection claim and provide sufficient evidence to establish it; the Tribunal is not obliged to assist.

S 5AAA of the Act imposes on the applicant the responsibility of specifying all particulars of their claim to be a person to whom Australia has protection obligations, and to provide sufficient evidence to establish the claim. The Tribunal is not obliged to assist in specifying the applicant's claims or to establish or assist in establishing a claim.

At the time of decision, the applicant does not continue to meet the requirements of either cl.802.212 or cl.802.214, and therefore the requirements of cl.802.221(2) are not met.

Fung (Migration)

Before: Kira Raif

Key holding

For a Subclass 802 Child visa, an applicant over 18 must have been undertaking full-time study since turning 18 or within a reasonable time after Year 12, and must continue to meet that requirement at the time of decision.

At the time of application, the applicant must have, since turning 18, or within 6 months or a reasonable time after completing the equivalent of year 12 in the Australian school system, been undertaking a full-time course of study at an educational institution leading to the award of a professional, trade or vocational qualification: cl 802.214(1)(c).

Key holding

For a Subclass 309 spouse visa, the applicant must be in a married relationship satisfying each element in s 5F(2)(a)-(d) at both the time of application and the time of decision.

Persons in a married relationship must be married to each other under a marriage that is valid for the purposes of the Act, there must be a mutual commitment to a shared life as a married couple to the exclusion of all others, the relationship must be genuine and continuing, and the couple must live together, or not live separately and apart on a permanent basis: s.5F(2)(a)-(d).

2116198 (Refugee)

1 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4430Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Sue Zelinka
The Tribunal reviewed the refusal of a protection visa to a Nigerian citizen who claimed to fear the Sea Dogs cult after his father’s death and claimed he could not access diabetes treatment in Nigeria. The Tribunal found the cult claims not credible and no real chance of serious harm, and rejected the diabetes-related particular social group and complementary protection claims. It affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

A shared medical condition such as Type 2 diabetes in Nigeria is not, without more, a particular social group because it lacks innate or immutable characteristics.

The Tribunal does not accept the applicant's construction of a particular social group constituted by 'Adults with Type 2 Diabetes in Nigeria'. In the Tribunal's view, this is not a group with innate or immutable characteristics seeing that diabetes is not an inborn characteristic nor one that is unchangeable.

LI (Migration)

1 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3919Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Mary Sheargold
This Tribunal decision concerned an application for review of a refusal to grant a Student (Temporary) (Class TU) Subclass 500 visa. The review was lodged one day late after the applicant’s former migration agent allegedly failed to notify her of the refusal until 21 May 2024 and gave incorrect lodgement advice. The Tribunal found it had no jurisdiction because the statutory deadline could not be extended.
Key holding

The Tribunal cannot waive the statutory time limit for lodging a review application, even where the applicant was late because her migration agent delayed notifying her of the refusal.

While the Tribunal appreciates the difficult circumstances the applicant faces, having relied on an authorised representative to receive news of her visa refusal but failing to notify her until it was too late, the Tribunal must emphasise that it has no discretion to waive the statutory time frame for making an application in these circumstances.

1 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3728Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewMigration

The review application was therefore not properly made and could not proceed to merits review.
Key holding

A refusal of an Electronic Travel Authority (Class UD) visa is not reviewable by the Tribunal, so the Tribunal lacks jurisdiction over an application seeking review of such a refusal.

Decisions to refuse to grant an ETA are not reviewable by the Tribunal because they are applied for while the applicant is outside the migration zone or in immigration clearance, and do not include a criterion for a relevant sponsorship or visiting a relative.

For a Subclass 820 visa at the time of decision, the applicant must continue to be the spouse or de facto partner of the sponsoring partner unless a cl 820.221 exception applies.

At the time of decision, the applicant must continue to be the spouse or de facto partner of the sponsoring partner: 820.221(1). Exceptions apply in certain circumstances where the sponsor has died, or family violence has occurred or there are certain court orders or responsibilities in relation to children.

Kaur (Migration)

Key holding

Under PIC 4020(4), PIC 4020(1) and (2) may be waived only if there are compelling circumstances affecting Australia’s interests or compassionate or compelling circumstances affecting an Australian citizen, permanent resident or eligible New Zealand citizen that justify granting the visa.

The requirements of PIC 4020(1) and (2) may be waived where there are compelling circumstances that affect the interests of Australia, or where there are compassionate or compelling circumstances that affect the interests of an Australian citizen, an Australian permanent resident, or an eligible New Zealand citizen (as defined in reg 1.03), that justify the granting of the visa.

Asamoah (Migration)

1 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3649Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Edward Howard
In Asamoah (Migration) [2024] AATA 3649, the Tribunal affirmed refusal of a Child (Migrant) (Class AH) Subclass 101 visa to a 24-year-old Ghanaian applicant relying on a 2014 customary adoption. The Tribunal found the adoption did not meet the definition in reg 1.04 because the child-parent relationship with the review applicant was not shown to be significantly closer than with his long-term carer, Esther, and formal adoption in Ghana was available and reasonably practicable.
Key holding

A customary adoption does not satisfy r 1.04(2)(c)(i) where formal adoption was available and reasonably practicable and the adopter chose customary adoption for personal convenience.

Further, the Tribunal finds that formal adoption was available in Ghana at the relevant time, that the review applicant was aware of this, that he was provided with alternatives in the adoption process and made a choice based on personal convenience. Further, the Tribunal finds that there is no evidence to suggest that formal adoption process was not reasonably practicable in the circumstances.

Tuicake (Migration)

1 Oct 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3646Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Kira Raif
Ms Tuicake applied for a Child (Residence) (Class BT) Subclass 802 visa. The delegate refused because she did not meet the study requirements for applicants over 18 in cl 802.214. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal, finding the cl 802.214 criteria did not continue to be met at decision and declining to refer the matter for ministerial intervention.
Key holding

Where the full-time study criterion in cl 802.214(1)(c) applies to an adult Subclass 802 applicant, it must continue to be met at the time of decision under cl 802.221(2)(b), assessed by looking at the whole period from commencement of study to decision.

Where cl 802.214(1)(c) applies, it must continue to be met at the time of decision: cl 802.221(2)(b). For this purpose, the decision-maker must look at the time period from the commencement of study until the time of decision and ask whether, characterised as a whole, the visa applicant's conduct in that period warrants the conclusion that they have been undertaking relevant study: Hussain v MIBP [2017] FCCA 3247.

Singh and Minister for Immigration, Citizenship and Multicultural Affairs (Citizenship)

30 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3544Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Mrs Singh, a 72-year-old New Zealand citizen, applied for Australian citizenship by conferral but had been an Australian permanent resident for less than the 12 months required before her application. The Tribunal affirmed the delegate’s refusal because she did not satisfy the residence requirement and no statutory discretion applied.
Key holding

Eligibility under s 21(3)(c) requires satisfaction of the applicable residence or defence service requirement at the time of application, and the Tribunal cannot waive that requirement through a general discretion absent the statutory conditions.

However, these are not factors that assist Mrs Singh in meeting the residence requirements, or that allow them to be waived. Mrs Singh did not satisfy the general residence requirement (see section 22) or the special residence requirement (see section 22A or 22B), or the defence service requirement (see section 23), at the time she applied for citizenship, and so she does not meet the eligibility requirement of subsection 21(3) of the Citizenship Act.

Lin (Migration)

29 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3701Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Tegen Downes
Mr Lin sought review of a refusal to grant a Partner (Temporary) (Class UK) visa (Subclass 820) on the basis of a claimed de facto relationship. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal because he provided no substantive relationship evidence and did not respond to the Tribunal’s requests, including a s 359(2) invitation. It found the de facto criteria in s 5CB(2) were not met at application or decision.
Key holding

Where a review applicant fails to respond to a s 359(2) invitation, the Tribunal may proceed without a hearing because the applicant loses any entitlement to appear.

In these circumstances, s 359C of the Act applies and, pursuant to s 360(3), the applicant is not entitled to appear before the Tribunal. The effect of s 363A of the Act is that if a review applicant has no entitlement to a hearing, the Tribunal has no power to permit him or her to appear: Hasran v MIAC [2010] FCAFC 40.

Casiano (Migration)

27 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3830Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Kira Raif
In Casiano (Migration) [2024] AATA 3830, Mr Casiano sought review of a refusal of an Other Family (Residence) (Class BU) visa as a secondary applicant and member of his mother’s family unit. The Tribunal considered whether he was a dependent child of the primary visa applicant, given he was over 23 and there was no evidence of incapacity. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

For a child aged 23 or older to be a member of the family unit as a dependent child, the Tribunal must be satisfied they are wholly or substantially reliant on the family head for financial support because they are incapacitated for work due to loss of bodily or mental functions.

If the applicant has turned 23, then the Tribunal must be satisfied that they are dependent on the family head because they meet reg 1.05A(1)(b), i.e. that they are wholly or substantially reliant on the other person for financial support because the first person is incapacitated for work due to the total or partial loss of the first person's bodily or mental functions.

1915886 (Refugee)

26 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4425Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Troy Barty
The Tribunal affirmed refusal of a protection visa for a Thai citizen who claimed financial hardship, family debts and past threats from a loan shark. The Tribunal accepted the family’s debts but found no Convention-related persecution and no real chance of serious or significant harm. In plain terms, economic difficulty and low-level loan-shark harassment did not meet protection thresholds.
Key holding

General financial hardship, difficulty repaying debts and lower income do not amount to persecution for a Convention reason unless the feared harm is for race, religion, nationality, political opinion or membership of a particular social group.

In relation to the applicant's fear of economic harm, being his family's financial situation, difficulty repaying debts, difficulty finding work and lower income, the Tribunal finds that the harms feared by the applicant are not for the essential and significant reason of his race, religion, nationality, political opinion or membership of a particular social group. As a result, the applicant does not meet the criteria in s5J(1)(a) and therefore the applicant does not satisfy s 36(2)(a).

2216142 (Refugee)

26 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4088Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Suhad Dutra
This is an AATA protection visa review for a Bangladeshi applicant who claimed to fear serious harm in Bangladesh from a family land dispute and politically connected relatives. The Tribunal was not satisfied he met either the refugee or complementary protection criteria.
Key holding

Although there is no formal onus in administrative review, an applicant must provide the relevant facts in sufficient detail for the decision-maker to establish them; the Tribunal is not obliged to make the applicant’s case or accept allegations uncritically.

It remains for the applicant to satisfy the Tribunal that all of the statutory elements are made out. Although the concept of onus of proof is not appropriate to administrative inquiries and decision-making, the relevant facts of the individual case will have to be supplied by the applicant himself or herself, in as much detail as is necessary to enable the examiner to establish the relevant facts.

26 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3509Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
David Verrill asked the Administrative Appeals Tribunal to disqualify Senior Member R Bellamy from his remitted review of a visa cancellation under s 501 of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth). He alleged actual and apprehended bias arising from adverse media about an unrelated decision, criticisms of his former lawyers, a prior decision concerning another non-citizen, and the allocation of other cases to the member. The Tribunal refused the disqualification application.
Key holding

For apprehended bias, a decision-maker is not disqualified unless a fair-minded lay observer might reasonably apprehend that the decision-maker might not bring an impartial mind to the question to be decided.

That test is whether a fair-minded lay observer might reasonably apprehend that a judge might not bring an impartial mind to the resolution of the question the judge is required to decide.

Dumbaya (Migration)

25 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3844Tribunal reviewVisa integrityVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Peter Emmerton
This was a review of a delegate’s refusal of a Child (Migrant) (Class AH) visa for Mr Eddie Sheka Sidique. The Tribunal found that a purported Sierra Leone High Court order and other information supporting the visa were bogus or false, so the applicant did not meet PIC 4020(1), and it declined to waive PIC 4020. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

PIC 4020(1) is engaged by a bogus document or false or misleading material information where some person has engaged in fraud or deception, even if the applicant did not know the information was untrue.

While PIC 4020 refers to information that is false, in the sense of purposely untrue, it is not necessary for the Minister (or the Tribunal on review) to conclude that the applicant was aware the information was purposely untrue in order for PIC 4020 to be engaged. However, an element of fraud or deception by some person is necessary to attract the operation of the provision: Trivedi v MIBP [2014] FCAFC 42.

McMillan (Migration)

25 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3713Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Michael Ison
The Tribunal reviewed the refusal of Mr McMillan’s Special Eligibility (Class CB) Former Resident (Subclass 151) visa. Although he had been an Australian permanent resident for most of his childhood, he spent 3,269 days in the migration zone before turning 18 and needed 3,288 days. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal but referred his circumstances to the Minister under s 351.
Key holding

For the long residence applicant definition, time spent physically outside the migration zone as a minor is not constructively treated as time in the migration zone.

The Tribunal is not persuaded, despite the considerable efforts of the representative, that it can reasonably interpret travel overseas as a minor as constructively being time 'in the migration zone' for the purposes of an applicant being eligible to be a long residence applicant for a Former Resident visa.

2115044 (Refugee)

24 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3976Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Sue Zelinka
The applicant, a Macedonian citizen, sought review of a refusal of a protection visa based on feared harm from family and an alleged criminal gang after his secret marriage to his first cousin became known. The Tribunal accepted the marriage and societal stigma but found no real chance of serious harm, given the divorce, his father’s reconciliation, his second marriage, and that “Komiti” was a football supporters’ club. It affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

A particular social group claim cannot succeed where the asserted group is not cognisable because its defining marriage is unlawful in the receiving country, or where the group is defined only by a shared fear of persecution or family harm.

The Tribunal rejects this formulation noting that there is no such group because first cousins, by law, cannot marry in Macedonia and therefore there cannot be a large, cognisable group of them. Any other variation of particular social group involving victims of harm at family hands is also rejected because a particular social group cannot be constituted by persons bound only by fear of persecution.

Chopra (Migration)

24 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3690Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Susan Hoffman
The Tribunal reviewed a refusal of Business Skills (Provisional) (Class EB) visas, Subclass 188 (Business Innovation and Investment (Provisional)), in the Business Innovation stream. The applicant relied on a partnership business and a sole trader business to meet the turnover and main business requirements. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal because the relevant turnover was below AUD 500,000 once government tax collections were excluded.
Key holding

For business visa turnover, amounts collected on behalf of a third party and passed on, such as motor vehicle government tax receipts, are not included in annual turnover; only the commission or fee for that collection service is included.

The Tribunal has already discussed why monies collected on behalf of a third party is not the same as turnover, as those monies are passed onto the third party. They generate an income (turnover) for a business through commission earned or fees charged for performing that collection service.

Cai (Migration)

23 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3835Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Paul Windsor
Mr Cai applied for a Visitor (Class FA) subclass 600 visa in the Tourist stream. The delegate refused the visa because it was not satisfied he genuinely intended to stay temporarily in Australia under cl 600.211. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal, finding his long continuous stay and unsupported medical claims indicated he was using the visitor visa to establish de facto residence.
Key holding

To satisfy cl 600.211, the applicant must positively satisfy the Tribunal of a genuine intention to stay temporarily for the visa purpose; a long continuous stay without a credible departure timeframe or supporting medical evidence can show the visitor visa is being used for de facto residence.

Given the applicant has been in Australia continuously since January 2020 and could not give any time-frame within which he might feel well enough to depart Australia, the Tribunal considers the applicant is seeking to use the subclass 600 Visitor visa to establish de facto residence in Australia. Consequently, the Tribunal is not satisfied that the visa applicant genuinely intends to stay temporarily in Australia for the purpose for which the visa would be granted.

1727642 (Refugee)

20 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4409Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Mary-Ann Cooper
This is an AATA review of a delegate’s refusal of a protection visa to an Indonesian citizen who claimed fear of a moneylender and debt collectors over an unpaid business loan, plus general economic hardship in Indonesia. The Tribunal accepted his debt account as credible but found the feared harm was not for a Convention reason and did not meet complementary protection thresholds. It affirmed the visa refusal.
Key holding

Harm feared from private debt collectors is not persecution for a Convention reason where the essential and significant reason is coercion to repay an unpaid debt.

This claimed harm arises because the applicant owes money that he has not repaid and/or is unable to repay. This is the essential and significant reason for the harm that the applicant claims he will suffer, not for one of the reasons listed in s 5J(1)(a) of the Act (s 5J(4)(a)).

Gurung (Migration)

19 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3487Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Frank Russo
The Tribunal considered a review application by Mr Ugen Gurung, a secondary applicant for a Subclass 500 Student visa refused because he did not meet cl 500.311. The Tribunal found he was outside the migration zone when the review application was made, so it lacked jurisdiction to review his refusal. His wife’s separate review application had earlier been remitted with a direction that she met cl 500.212.

For a decision described in s 338(2) of the Migration Act, the Tribunal has jurisdiction only if the person applying for review was physically present in the migration zone when the review application was made.

In the case of a decision described in s.338(2), an application for review may only be made by a non-citizen who is the subject of the decision and is physically present in the migration zone when the application for review is made: s.347(2)(a) and (3).

1904483 (Refugee)

18 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3757Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Fraser Robertson
The Tribunal reviewed a delegate’s refusal to grant a protection visa to a Malaysian applicant. The applicant failed to attend the scheduled Tribunal hearing, so the application was dismissed under s 426A(1A)(b) of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth). As the applicant did not apply for reinstatement within 14 days, the Tribunal confirmed the dismissal and the decision under review was taken to be affirmed.
Key holding

Where an applicant’s review is dismissed for failure to attend a Tribunal hearing and the applicant does not apply for reinstatement within 14 days, the Tribunal must confirm the dismissal and the decision under review is taken to be affirmed.

As the applicant did not apply for reinstatement of the application within the 14 day period, the Tribunal must confirm the decision to dismiss the application. In these circumstances, the decision under review is taken to be affirmed.

2009973 (Refugee)

17 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4378Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Patricia Tyson
The Tribunal reviewed a refusal of a protection visa to a Fijian national who claimed economic hardship, inability to find work, political instability, drug-related pressure and separation from his Australian family if returned to Fiji. The applicant said he had not known about the original protection visa application, which was lodged by others and contained untrue claims. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal because it was not satisfied Australia owed protection obligations.
Key holding

Inability to find work or financial difficulty does not amount to persecution under s 5J unless it involves systematic and discriminatory conduct for a Convention reason.

However, even if it were the case that the applicant returned to his village and/or was unable to find work and faced financial difficulty, the applicant did not suggest and I am not satisfied on the evidence that this would involve systematic or discriminatory conduct, or be for one of the reasons in s 5J(1)(a). I am not satisfied the applicant has a well-founded fear of persecution in this regard.

Moodley (Migration)

17 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3992Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Kira Raif
The Tribunal reviewed refusal of a Child (Residence) (Class BT) visa, Subclass 802, for a UK applicant who was over 18. The delegate found he did not meet the study requirements because he was not studying when he applied. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal, finding he also was not studying at the time of decision and was working full-time.
Key holding

For a Subclass 802 Child visa applicant over 18, the required full-time study must have been commenced by the time of application, and reasons for not studying do not assist where no study was commenced or engaged in; the applicant must also be studying at the time of decision.

The Tribunal is of the view that the reasoning in Khan does not support the requirement for the decision-maker to assess the applicant's reasons for not studying in circumstances where no study was commenced or engaged in. It may be that if the visa applicant did commence the relevant course by the time the application for the visa was made, it would have been open to the Tribunal to consider the reasons for any break in studies.

Verbatim excerpt, AI-selected. Check the full text.

Before: Kira Raif
Ms Hannah Koroma sought review of a delegate’s refusal to grant Miss Alimatu Bangura a Child (Migrant) (Class AH) visa, Subclass 117 (Orphan Relative). The Tribunal affirmed the refusal because it was not satisfied the visa applicant was the sponsor’s niece/relative or an orphan relative under reg 1.14. Credibility and documentary concerns, together with inconclusive DNA results, were determinative.
Key holding

The visa applicant bears the burden of satisfying the decision-maker that the relevant relationship and visa criteria are met, and the Tribunal need not determine the precise nature of a claimed family relationship where the evidence is inconclusive.

It is not for the Tribunal to determine the precise nature of that relationship but for the visa applicant to satisfy the decision - maker that she meets the requirements for the grant of the visa.

This is a short migration review turning on the temporal requirements for the English test evidence.
Key holding

The Tribunal restated that it considers that the regulatory requirement contains a critical temporal element. Key holding

Clause 485.212(1)(a) requires the visa application to be accompanied by evidence that the applicant has undertaken a specified English language test and achieved the specified score within the specified period; absent such evidence, the criterion is not met.

Clause 485.212(1) requires that the application was accompanied by evidence that: the applicant has undertaken a language test specified in an instrument; and has achieved, within the period specified in the instrument, the score specified, in accordance with any specified requirements (cl 485.212(1)(a)); or the applicant holds a passport of a type specified by the Minister in an instrument (cl 485.212(1)(b)).

ZHANG (Migration)

16 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3488Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Robyn Anderson
This is an Administrative Appeals Tribunal (Migration & Refugee Division) review of a refusal to grant Business Innovation and Investment (Provisional) Subclass 188 visas in the Investor stream. The first-named applicant failed to provide evidence of the required net business and personal assets, and the second-named applicant did not satisfy secondary criteria. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

Where an applicant for a Subclass 188 Investor visa provides no evidence of the required net business and personal assets for the relevant period, the Tribunal cannot be satisfied that cl 188.245 is met and will affirm the refusal.

In such circumstances, the Tribunal cannot be satisfied that for the relevant period, the business and personal assets of the applicant, the applicant's spouse or de facto partner or the applicant and his spouse or de facto partner together had a net value of AUD2,250,000. Therefore, the Tribunal finds that the requirement under cl 188.245 of Schedule 2 to the Regulations is not met and that it is appropriate to affirm the decision under review in respect of the applicant.

Diplock and Minister for Immigration, Citizenship, and Multicultural Affairs (Citizenship)

16 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3281Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Jessica Diplock sought review of a delegate’s refusal to approve her Australian citizenship by conferral. The delegate found she did not satisfy s 21(2)(g) of the Australian Citizenship Act 2007 (Cth) and that the s 24(5) prohibition applied because she was offshore. The Administrative Appeals Tribunal affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

Under s 21(2)(g) of the Australian Citizenship Act 2007 (Cth), “likely” means probable rather than merely possible, so an applicant must show a real probability of residing in Australia or maintaining a close and continuing association with Australia if approved.

On the cumulative evidence, the Tribunal is satisfied that consistent with the meaning of 'likely' in CPI 11, although it is possible that the Applicant will reside in Australia in the future, it is not probable that she would do so.

2207647 (Refugee)

13 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4414Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Robert McLaughlin
This is a protection visa review concerning an Indonesian citizen of Chinese ethnicity who claimed to fear persecution in Indonesia due to ethnicity, religion, cultural practices, extortion and physical harm. The Tribunal found the applicant’s claims lacked credibility and did not accept that he faced a well-founded fear of persecution or a real risk of significant harm. The decision under review was affirmed.
Key holding

A claimed fear of persecution must not be speculative, and the applicant must provide sufficiently detailed material to enable the Tribunal to establish the relevant facts.

A fear of persecution is not 'well-founded' if it is merely assumed or if it is mere speculation. Although the concept of onus of proof is not appropriate to administrative inquiries and decision-making, the applicant must provide material, in as much detail as is necessary, to enable the Tribunal to establish the relevant facts as claimed.

The Tribunal reviewed a refusal of a Student (Temporary) (Class TU) visa, subclass 500. The applicant had completed prior study but failed to provide evidence of current enrolment despite being given time to do so.
Key holding

For a Subclass 500 Student visa, cl 500.211(a) requires the applicant to be enrolled in a course of study at the time of the Tribunal’s decision.

Clause 500.211 relevantly requires that at the time of this decision the applicant is enrolled in a course of study: cl 500.211(a).

Key holding

Paul (Migration)

10 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3918Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Margie Bourke
The Tribunal affirmed refusal of an Aged Parent (Residence) (Class BP) (Subclass 804) visa because the applicant did not satisfy the balance of family test at the time of application. At application, she had one Australian-citizen child, one child in Portugal, and one child in Australia on a temporary visa; the third child later became a permanent resident, but this did not cure the time-of-application failure. The Tribunal decided on the papers by consent and referred the matter to the Minister under s 351.
Key holding

A child who holds only a temporary visa at the time of application is not an eligible child for the balance of family test, which is assessed at the time of application.

The daughter Candida Shetty was the holder of a temporary visa at the time of application, and therefore did not meet the definition of eligible child at the time of application for the purpose of the balance of family test. The Tribunal is satisfied that at the time of application the applicant had one eligible child, her son Edward Paul who was an Australian citizen.

Key holding

If an applicant's review is dismissed for non-appearance and no reinstatement application is made within 14 days, the Tribunal must confirm the dismissal and the underlying decision is taken to be affirmed.

As the applicants did not apply for reinstatement of the application within the 14 days period, the Tribunal must confirm the decision to dismiss the application. In these circumstances, the decisions under review are taken to be affirmed.

Martinez Ferrada (Migration)

An application for a Student visa or a Student Guardian visa should be refused if, after weighing up the applicant's circumstances, immigration history and any other relevant matter, the decision maker is not satisfied that the applicant genuinely intends a temporary stay in Australia.

Huynh (Migration)

9 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3522Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Justine Clarke
Huynh concerns a refusal of an Other Family (Migrant) (Class BO) visa, specifically the Subclass 115 (Remaining Relative) visa, to a Vietnamese applicant sponsored by her brother in Australia. Although the brother was accepted as an Australian relative usually resident in Australia, the applicant’s mother was usually resident in Vietnam and not an Australian citizen, permanent resident or eligible New Zealand citizen at the time of application.
Key holding

For a Subclass 115 visa, because cl 115.221 requires the applicant to continue to satisfy cl 115.211, a failure to meet reg 1.15(1)(c) at the time of application cannot be cured by later circumstances.

In effect, the use of the word 'continues' in this clause necessitates that a visa applicant has met the criterion in cl 115.211. Clause 115.211 is a time of application criterion which requires that, at the time a visa applicant applies for the Subclass 115 visa, they are 'a remaining relative of an Australian relative for the applicant'.
