Hampson and Minister for Immigration, Citizenship, Migrant Services and Multicultural Affairs (Citizenship)

9 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3264Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
The applicant, a UK-born minor, applied for Australian citizenship by descent through his mother, an Australian citizen by descent. A delegate refused the application because the mother had not been present in Australia for the required two years (730 days) before the application. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal, finding no discretion to overcome the shortfall.
Key holding

Under s 16(2)(b)(i) of the Australian Citizenship Act 2007 (Cth), the two-year presence requirement is mandatory and the Minister or Tribunal has no discretion to approve citizenship by descent if it is not met.

There is no discretion available to the Minister with respect to an Applicant not meeting the requirements of section 16(2) of the Act. If these prescriptive legislative requirements are not able to be met, the citizenship application cannot be approved.

For complementary protection, a risk of significant harm faced by the population generally and not personally by the applicant is taken not to be a real risk under s 36(2B)(c).

The Tribunal also notes that section 36(2B) qualifies s 36(2)(aa) by setting out circumstances in which there is taken not to be a real risk that an applicant will suffer significant harm in a country and it includes where the real risk is one faced by the population of the country generally and is not faced by an applicant personally.

Belmont and Minister for Immigration and Multicultural Affairs (Migration)

6 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3512Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
Mr Belmont, a New Zealand citizen whose visa was mandatorily cancelled under s 501 of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth), sought disqualification of the Tribunal member constituted to hear his remitted revocation review. He alleged actual and apprehended bias arising from the member’s criticisms of his former lawyers in an unrelated decision, media articles about that decision, and her prior decisions. The Tribunal refused the disqualification application.
Key holding

A decision-maker is disqualified for apprehended bias only if a fair-minded lay observer might reasonably apprehend that the decision-maker might not bring an impartial mind to the question to be decided.

That test is whether a fair-minded lay observer might reasonably apprehend that a judge might not bring an impartial mind to the resolution of the question the judge is required to decide.

Vani and Minister for Citizenship and Multicultural Affairs (Citizenship)

6 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3180Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Stanley Vani applied for Australian citizenship by descent based on his father’s claimed Aboriginal Australian status, which he said would avoid the effect of s 65(1) of the PNG Constitution. The Tribunal found the Applicant’s father did not satisfy the tripartite test for Aboriginality as at PNG Independence Day, 16 September 1975. The delegate’s refusal of citizenship by descent was affirmed.
Key holding

To establish Aboriginal Australian status, a person must satisfy the tripartite test of biological descent, self-identification, and mutual recognition by elders or others with traditional authority, with mutual recognition requiring authority sourced in pre-European settlement norms.

Membership of the indigenous people depends on biological descent from the indigenous people and on mutual recognition of a particular person's membership by that person and by the elders or other persons enjoying traditional authority among those people.

Yeh (Migration)

5 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3415Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Jade Murphy
The Applicant sought review of a delegate’s refusal of a Student (Temporary) (Class TU) Subclass 500 visa. The Tribunal found it had no jurisdiction because the review application was lodged on 10 January 2024, one day after the 21-day review period expired. A defect in the Department’s notification letter did not invalidate the notification because the Tribunal found substantial compliance.
Key holding

A notification containing a deemed-receipt error remains valid if it substantially complies with content requirements and causes no substantial prejudice, so the statutory review period runs from deemed receipt.

The Tribunal has considered the content of the refusal notification letter and considers that although there is an error in the notification, the notification is still valid because it substantially complies with the content requirements and the failure to strictly comply does not cause substantial prejudice to the applicant's rights.

1918782 (Refugee)

4 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3964Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Kathleen Timbs
The applicant, a Chinese citizen, sought a protection visa claiming he owed money from an online lending platform and feared being killed by loan-shark debt collectors in China. The Tribunal decided the review on the papers after the applicant declined a hearing. It affirmed the delegate’s refusal because the claims lacked sufficient detail to establish refugee or complementary protection criteria.
Key holding

An applicant bears the onus of providing sufficient evidence to satisfy the Tribunal of every statutory element of the refugee or complementary protection criteria; mere assertions of fear or risk are insufficient.

Similarly, that an applicant claims to face a real risk of significant harm does not establish that such a risk exists, or that the harm feared amounts to 'significant harm'. It is for the applicant to provide evidence that satisfies the Tribunal that all the statutory elements for one or more of the criteria are made out.

2308191 (Migration)

4 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3820Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Member Nathan Goetz
The Tribunal reviewed a refusal of Visitor (class FA) Sponsored Family (subclass 600) visas for the review applicant’s parents. The delegate refused because the applicants did not satisfy the genuine temporary stay criterion in cl 600.211. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal, finding the applicants’ oral evidence inconsistent with the review applicant’s protection visa claims and that their claimed intention to stay temporarily should be given no weight.
Key holding

Under cl 600.211, the Tribunal may give no weight to an applicant’s claimed genuine intention to stay temporarily where their oral evidence is inconsistent with the review applicant’s protection claims and shows a flexible approach to the truth.

The fact that the visa applicants provided oral evidence inconsistent with the protection claims advanced by the review applicant satisfies the Tribunal that they have a flexible approach to the truth, and that the Tribunal should not give any weight to their claimed intention to travel to Australia temporarily for the purpose for which the visa is issued.

Sharma (Migration)

4 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3406Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Penelope Hunter
This is a review of a refusal to grant a Student (Temporary) (Class TU) Subclass 500 visa. The delegate refused because the applicant, an Indian citizen, was not satisfied to be a genuine temporary entrant under cl 500.212. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal.

Refusal is upheld where the Tribunal is not satisfied, after considering Direction No 108 factors as a whole, that the applicant genuinely intends to stay in Australia temporarily under cl 500.212(a).

Hughes and Minister for Immigration, Citizenship, and Multicultural Affairs (Migration)

4 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3158Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
Michael Hughes, a 58-year-old New Zealand/United Kingdom citizen, had his subclass 189 visa mandatorily cancelled after being convicted of indecent dealing with a child under 13 and sentenced to 20 months’ imprisonment. He sought review of a delegate’s decision not to revoke that cancellation under s 501CA(4) of the Migration Act 1958 (Cth). The Tribunal affirmed the non-revocation decision, applying Direction no. 110 and refusing to go behind the conviction and sentence.
Key holding

In a s 501CA review, the Tribunal cannot go behind the essential facts of the conviction and sentence that founded the mandatory cancellation, and an applicant bears a heavy onus to challenge those facts.

In those circumstances, it is not open to the Tribunal to go behind the essential facts of that conviction and sentence. Moreover, the Applicant has not discharged what would otherwise be his "heavy onus" to challenge the facts upon which the conviction and sentence are necessarily based as set out above.

2429896 (Migration)

3 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3497Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Antonio Dronjic
The applicant, a Nepalese citizen in immigration detention, applied for a Bridging E (Class WE) visa based on a pending protection visa application. The Tribunal found the visa ground was met but refused the visa because it was not satisfied he would abide by conditions, including no work, reporting, address notification and no criminal conduct, regardless of any security. The Tribunal affirmed the delegate’s refusal.
Key holding

For a Subclass 050 Bridging (General) visa, cl 050.223 requires the Tribunal to be satisfied the applicant will abide by conditions; if not satisfied regardless of any security, the criterion is not met.

If the Tribunal is satisfied that the applicant will abide by the conditions if security of a particular amount is required, the applicant meets cl 050.223. However, if not satisfied that the applicant will comply with the conditions, regardless of any security that may be imposed, cl 050.223 is not met.

2015142 (Refugee)

2 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4359Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
Before: Dr Greg Weeks
This is a Tribunal review of a delegate’s refusal to grant protection visas to a married Slovenian couple. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal because the primary applicant admitted the protection visa application was made to “buy time” and did not fear harm in Slovenia, and his claims about Muslim migrants and Catholicism were not accepted.
Key holding

The “real risk” element of complementary protection applies the same standard as the “real chance” test for well-founded fear of persecution in the Refugee Convention definition.

The 'real risk' element of the complementary protection criterion has been held by a Full Court of the Federal Court of Australia to impose the same standard as the 'real chance' test applicable to the assessment of 'well-founded fear' in the Refugee Convention definition.

1902267 (Refugee)

2 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3930Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Mark Oakman
The Tribunal reviewed the refusal of protection visas to an Irish primary applicant and two children, plus a UK applicant partner. The Irish applicants had left Australia and did not respond to an invitation to comment, while the UK partner did not attend the hearing and made no separate claims. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal because the protection visa criteria were not satisfied.
Key holding

A protection visa cannot be granted unless the applicant is in Australia, because s 36(2) requires the applicant to be a non-citizen in Australia.

Under s 65(1) a visa may be granted only if the decision maker is satisfied that the prescribed criteria for the visa have been satisfied. So far as is relevant to this matter, s 36(2) of the Act provides that a criterion for a protection visa is that the applicant for the visa is a non-citizen in Australia. This means that a protection visa may only be granted if the applicant is in Australia.

1909057 (Refugee)

1 Sep 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4185Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration

The AATA reviewed a delegate’s refusal of protection visas to a Nepali family—husband, wife and their Australian-born daughter—who claimed fear of harm in Nepal because of their inter-caste/inter-religious marriage. Key holding

Accordingly, the delegate found that s 36(3) applies to the applicants and that they have statutory effective protection in a third country.

August 2024 (17)

Khendo Sherpa (Migration)

31 Aug 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3393Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Christine Kannis
The Tribunal affirmed cancellation of Ms Dawa Khendo Sherpa’s Subclass 500 (Student) visa (Class TU) because she was not enrolled in a full-time registered course from 11 March 2021 to 8 June 2023. The Tribunal found breach of condition 8202(2)(a) and, after considering discretionary factors, declined to set aside cancellation.
Key holding

Condition 8202(2)(a) imposes a continuing requirement that a student visa holder remain enrolled in a full-time registered course, so even a temporary gap in enrolment breaches the condition.

Condition 8202(2)(a) requires that the holder be enrolled and the enrolment requirement is a continuing requirement and does not allow the visa holder to cease to be enrolled in a course, even to the extent of a temporary gap in enrolment.

2405987 (Refugee)

30 Aug 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4154Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Khanh Hoang
The Tribunal reviewed a delegate’s refusal of a protection visa to a Vanuatu citizen who claimed family land inheritance disputes, political tensions and black magic threats. It accepted some family tension and threats but found he could safely return to Port Vila, where he had lived for about a decade, and affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

A well-founded fear of persecution requires a real chance that is not remote or far-fetched, and the chance may be below 50 per cent.

A 'real chance' is one that is not remote or insubstantial or a far-fetched possibility. A person can have a well-founded fear of persecution even though the possibility of the persecution occurring is well below 50 per cent: Chan Yee Kin v MIEA (1989) 169 CLR 379.

Araoz (Migration)

30 Aug 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3174Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: David Barker
Mrs Claudia Araoz sought review of a delegate’s refusal of a Student (Temporary) (Class TU) visa. Although the delegate refused for not being a genuine temporary entrant, the Tribunal considered whether she met the current enrolment requirement. Because she did not respond to Tribunal invitations and PRISMS showed no current Confirmation of Enrolment, the Tribunal affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

The Tribunal treats PRISMS as a reliable record of a visa applicant’s enrolment unless there is specific evidence to the contrary.

While it is possible providers may not update PRISMS as required, the Tribunal accepts that PRISMS is a reliable record of enrolments unless there is specific evidence to the contrary in relation to a particular case. There is no such evidence in this case.

2429454 (Migration)

30 Aug 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3172Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Anne Grant
The Tribunal reviewed a delegate’s refusal to grant a Bridging E (Class WE) visa, where the applicant wanted to remain in Australia for criminal hearings and relied on having acceptable arrangements to depart. The Tribunal was not satisfied he met cl 050.212(2) or any other alternative criterion, and affirmed the refusal. It also found he did not meet Subclass 051 criteria.
Key holding

For cl 050.212(2), a bare assertion of third-party financial assistance, without evidence of the supporter’s willingness and capacity, is insufficient to establish acceptable arrangements to depart Australia.

A simple assertion that someone will help with flight purchases and financial support, without any confirmation demonstrating that the supporting person is willing and has the capacity to do so is insufficient, in my view, to be considered 'appropriate arrangements' for departure.

1829009 (Refugee)

Employment discrimination causing only relative economic disadvantage, without serious harm as defined in s 5J(5), does not establish a well-founded fear of persecution.

Thus, the Tribunal finds that the level of harm the applicant will experience does not rise to the level of constituting serious harm as outlined in s 5J(5) of the Act.

2210099 (Refugee)

29 Aug 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3931Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Fraser Robertson
This is an AATA review of a delegate’s refusal to grant protection visas to a married Macedonian couple. They claimed to fear harm from a March 2018 armed attack allegedly linked to Applicant 1’s brief service in the 2001 Macedonian conflict, plus Applicant 2’s mental health and work capacity. The Tribunal accepted some background but rejected the claimed attack as implausible and affirmed the refusal.
Key holding

A decision-maker assessing complementary protection may rely on relevant findings made in the refugee assessment where the factual bases overlap.

To the extent that the factual bases for claims under s 36(2)(a) and s 36(2)(aa) overlap, a decision-maker, when considering the complementary protection criterion under s 36(2)(aa), is entitled to refer to and rely on any relevant findings the decision-maker made when considering the refugee criterion under s 36(2)(a).

Latu (Migration)

29 Aug 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3132Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Meena Sripathy
The Tribunal affirmed the refusal of a Child (Residence) (Class BT) visa (Subclass 802) to an applicant who was 18 at the time of application. The sponsor stepfather held Family Law Act sole parental responsibility orders after the applicant’s mother died, but there was no adoption and the Tribunal found the migration criteria were not met. The Tribunal nevertheless referred the matter for Ministerial intervention based on compelling family circumstances.
Key holding

An applicant over 18 at the time of application cannot satisfy the r 1.03 step-child definition for a Subclass 802 visa where reliance is placed on being the child of a former spouse/de facto partner, even if the sponsor holds a Family Law Act sole parental responsibility order.

He was 18 years at the time of application, therefore he does not meet the requirements of the definition of step child in subparagraph (b) of the definition in r.1.03, despite the Family Law Act 1975 court order dated 24 February 2020 which gave the sponsor sole parental responsibility for him.

2411479 (Refugee)

28 Aug 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3799Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Peter Katsambanis
The Tribunal found it had no jurisdiction to review a refusal of a protection visa because the review application was lodged late. The delegate refused the visa on 6 April 2024, but the applicant did not lodge her review until 10 May 2024, outside the 28-day period.
Key holding

For a protection visa review where the applicant is not in immigration detention, the application must be lodged within 28 days starting on the day of notification; if lodged late, the Tribunal has no jurisdiction.

As the applicant was not in immigration detention on the day the applicant was notified of the decision, an application for review of the decision had to be made within 28 days, commencing on that day: reg 4.31(2) of the Migration Regulations 1994 (Cth) (the Regulations).

Barik (Migration)

28 Aug 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3417Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Sheridan Aster
The Tribunal considered an application to review a refusal to grant a Training (Class GF) Subclass 407 visa. Because the associated nomination had been refused and the applicant was not nominated by an approved sponsor at the time of the review application, the Tribunal found the visa refusal was not a reviewable decision. The application was therefore dismissed for lack of jurisdiction.
Key holding

A refusal of a Training (Subclass 407) visa is reviewable only if, at the time of the review application, the applicant has an approved sponsor nomination or has a pending review of the nomination refusal.

A decision to refuse a Training visa is reviewable if the applicant made the visa application while in the migration zone and the applicant is nominated by an approved sponsor at the time the application for review of the visa refusal is made or an application for review of a decision not to approve the nomination has been made but at the time the application for review of the visa refusal is made, review of the decision is pending.

Goncalves De Sa (Migration)

28 Aug 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3350Tribunal reviewVisa nominationVisa refusal reviewCharacter testMigration
Before: Peter Emmerton
The Tribunal affirmed refusal of Employer Nomination (Permanent) (Class EN) Subclass 186 visas in the Temporary Residence Transition stream because the primary applicant, a chef, could not demonstrate competent English at the time of application. Although he spoke English well at hearing and had substantial culinary experience, his IELTS results and Portuguese passport did not meet the prescribed criteria, and the Tribunal had no discretion.
Key holding

For a Subclass 186 TRT visa, competent English must be established at the time of application through a specified test score or specified passport, failing which cl 186.222(a) is not satisfied and the Tribunal has no discretion.

Since neither 1.15C(1) nor 1.15C(2) are satisfied, 1.15C is not satisfied. As 1.15C is not satisfied, the Tribunal finds the applicant did not have competent English at the time of lodging the visa application, and therefore does not satisfy 186.222(a).

Jiang (Migration)

28 Aug 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 3198Tribunal reviewVisa refusal reviewMigration
Before: Tania Flood
The Tribunal affirmed refusal of Ms Jiang’s Visitor (Class FA) Subclass 600 visa because she applied after her substantive visa expired. It found the late application resulted from forgetfulness and inadequate reminders, not factors beyond her control under Schedule 3 criterion 3004(c). The applicant was also offshore at the time of decision, so cl 600.411 was not met.
Key holding

Under criterion 3004(c), whether the applicant ceased to hold a substantive visa because of factors beyond their control is assessed subjectively and practically, and forgetfulness or failure to take available compliance measures will not ordinarily qualify.

The test is in that sense "subjective" rather than being directed at deciding what would have been beyond the control of an abstract or "reasonable" person. A second point is that what is "beyond control" should be tested by considering whether the happening of the event was or was not within the control of the applicant in a practical or realistic sense.

2411742 (Refugee)

27 Aug 2024Administrative Review Tribunal[2024] AATA 4375Tribunal reviewVisa cancellationVisa refusal reviewVisa integrityMigration
Before: Damian Creedon
The applicant is an older South Korean citizen who claimed protection in Australia because he feared being unable to find work in South Korea due to age discrimination and long absence. The Tribunal affirmed the refusal of his protection visa, finding South Korea offers effective protection against age discrimination and that his economic/employment concerns did not meet protection criteria.
Key holding

Effective protection measures available from the receiving State can negate a well-founded fear of persecution under s 5J(2).

The Tribunal finds, therefore, that South Korea can offer effective protection to its citizens against age discrimination. It follows, and the Tribunal finds, that effective protection measures against age discrimination are available to the applicant in South Korea, and that therefore he does not have a well-founded fear of persecution on that ground: s 5J(2).
